Separation and Definability in Fragments of Two-Variable First-Order Logic with Counting
1 st Given Name Surname, 2 nd Given Name Surname, 3 rd Given Name Surname, 4 th Given Name Surname, 5 th Given Name Surname, 6 th Given Name Surname, Louwe Kuijer, Tony Tan, Frank Wolter, Michael Zakharyaschev

Abstract
For fragments L L of first-order logic ( 𝖥𝖮 \mathsf{FO} ) with counting quantifiers, we consider the definability problem, which asks whether a given L L -formula can be equivalently expressed by a formula in some fragment of L L without counting, and the more general separation problem asking whether two mutually exclusive L L -formulas can be separated in some counting-free fragment of L L . We show that separation is undecidable for the two-variable fragment of 𝖥𝖮 \mathsf{FO} extended with counting quantifiers and for the graded modal logic with inverse, nominals and universal modality. On the other hand, if inverse or nominals are dropped, separation becomes coNExpTime - or 2ExpTime -complete, depending on whether the universal modality is present. In contrast, definability can often be reduced in polynomial time to validity in L L . We also consider uniform separation and show that it often behaves similarly to definability.



I
Introduction

Extensions of decidable fragments of first-order logic (
\mathsf{FO}
) with counting quantifiers
\exists^{\geq k}x
play an important role in computational logic. In modal logic (viewed as a fragment of
\mathsf{FO}
under the standard translation), counting was introduced in the early 1970s
[
1
,
2
]
as graded modalities
\Diamond^{\geq k}
and the graded modal logic,
\mathsf{GML}
, has been investigated and applied ever since
[
3
,
4
,
5
]
. In description logic (DL), counting quantifiers, known as qualified number restrictions, are a core constructor used in almost all standard languages
[
6
,
7
]
. A fundamental decidable fragment of
\mathsf{FO}
containing most decidable graded modal logics and standard DLs with qualified number restrictions is
\mathsf{C^{2}}
, the two-variable first-order logic (
\mathsf{FO^{2}}
) with counting quantifiers, in which satisfiability is
NExpTime
-complete
[
8
,
9
,
10
]
; see also
[
11
,
12
]
. Both
\mathsf{GML}
and
\mathsf{C^{2}}
have been recently shown to characterise exactly the expressive power of certain graph neural networks (GNNs)
[
13
,
14
]
.

While the expressive power of logics with counting such as
\mathsf{GML}
and
\mathsf{C^{2}}
is well-understood
[
15
,
16
,
17
]
, the problem of defining and separating formulas with counting by counting-free ones has been left largely unexplored. This is in contrast to logics with fixpoints, where the definability and separation of fixpoint formulas by those without fixpoints has been investigated extensively
[
18
,
19
,
20
,
21
,
22
]
, and to automata theory, where the definability and separation of languages defined using expressive devices by means of less powerful ones has been studied in depth, with countless applications to logics over linear orders and trees
[
23
,
24
,
25
,
26
]
.

The aim of this paper is to investigate the computational complexity of the following decision problems for most important logics
L
with counting and counting-free logics
L^{\prime}
:

L/L^{\prime}
-definability:

decide whether a given
L
-formula
\varphi
is equivalent to some
L^{\prime}
-formula, called an
L^{\prime}
-
definition
of
\varphi
;

L/L^{\prime}
-separation:

given two mutually exclusive
L
-formulas
\varphi
and
\psi
, decide whether there is an
L^{\prime}
-formula
\chi
—an
L^{\prime}
-separator
for
\varphi
and
\psi
—such that
\varphi\models\chi
and
\chi\models\neg\psi
;

uniform
L/L^{\prime}
-separation:

given an
L
-formula
\varphi
, decide if there is a logically strongest (modulo equivalence)
L^{\prime}
-formula
\chi
—a
uniform
L^{\prime}
separator
for
\varphi
—with
\varphi\models\chi
.

In DL, for instance,
\varphi
can be an ontology
\mathcal{O}
and
\psi
a concept
C
that is not satisfiable with respect to
\mathcal{O}
, both given in a DL
L
with number restrictions. Then a separator ontology
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
in a weaker, easier to comprehend language
L^{\prime}
without number restrictions could provide an easier ‘explanation’ of unsatisfiability as it inherits that
\mathcal{O}\models\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
and
C
is not satisfiable under
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
. In the context of concept learning
[
27
]
,
\varphi
and
\psi
can represent positive and negative examples for a target concept
C
. Then any separator in an appropriately
chosen language
L^{\prime}
without number restrictions could represent a qualitative rather than quantitative concept one aims to learn
[
28
,
29
]
.

Note that
(a)

L/L^{\prime}
-definability reduces to
L/L^{\prime}
-separation for
\varphi
and
\neg\varphi
,
(b)
an
L^{\prime}
-definition of
\varphi
is a uniform
L^{\prime}
-separator for
\varphi
, and
(c)
a uniform
L^{\prime}
-separator for
\varphi
is an
L/L^{\prime}
-separator for
\varphi
and any
L
-formula
\psi

L^{\prime}
-separable from
\varphi
.
The next example illustrates definability and uniform separation of
\mathsf{GML}
-formulas by those in plain modal logic
\mathsf{ML}
(the statements can be checked using Theorems
19
and
26
).

Example 1
.

The
\mathsf{GML}
-formula
\Diamond^{\geq n}p
is
\mathsf{ML}
-definable iff
n=1
. However,
\Diamond^{\geq n}p
always has a uniform
\mathsf{ML}
-separator, namely,
\Diamond p
. On the other hand, for any
n\geq 1
, the
\mathsf{GML}
-formulas
\Diamond^{\leq n}p
and
\Diamond^{=n}p
do not have a uniform
\mathsf{ML}
-separator.
The
\mathsf{GML}
-formula
\neg\Diamond^{=n}p
is not
\mathsf{ML}
-definable but has a uniform
\mathsf{ML}
-separator,
\top
, for any
n\geq 1
,
which means that
\neg\Diamond^{=n}p
does not have any non-trivial consequences in
\mathsf{ML}
.
\dashv

L/L^{\prime}
-separation can be regarded as a variant of Craig interpolation
[
30
]
, where some logical symbols (here, counting quantifiers) are not allowed to occur in interpolants. We also consider the bona fide generalisation of Craig interpolation:

Craig
L/L^{\prime}
-separation:

decide whether given
L
-formulas
\varphi
and
\psi
have an
L^{\prime}
-separator
\chi
that is built from common non-logical symbols of
\varphi
and
\psi
.

Example 2
.

Consider the
\mathsf{C^{2}}
-formulas
\varphi(x)=\exists^{=1}y\,R(x,y)
and

\psi(x)=\exists^{=1}y\,\big(R(x,y)\wedge A(y)\big)\wedge\exists^{=1}y\,\big(R(x,y)\wedge\neg A(y)\big)
.
Clearly,
\varphi\models\neg\psi
. The
\mathsf{FO^{2}}
-formula

\chi(x)=\forall y\,\big(R(x,y)\rightarrow A(y)\big)\vee\forall y\,\big(R(x,y)\rightarrow\neg A(y)\big)

separates
\varphi(x)
and
\psi(x)
.
For
\psi^{\prime}(x)=\exists^{=2}y\,R(x,y)
, we also have
\varphi\models\neg\psi^{\prime}
, but
\varphi(x)
and
\psi^{\prime}(x)
are not
\mathsf{FO^{2}}
-separable.
On the other hand, there is no Craig
\mathsf{FO^{2}}
-separator for
\varphi(x)
and
\psi(x)
, which has to be built from predicate
R
only, and so would separate
\varphi(x)
and
\psi^{\prime}(x)
as well.
\dashv

Our core decidability and complexity results are summarised in
Table
I
. Here,
\mathsf{FO^{2}}
admits equality,
\mathsf{FO^{2}_{\neq}}
is equality-free, and
\mathsf{ML}\!^{\mathsf{e}}
and
\mathsf{GML}\!^{\mathsf{e}}
denote extensions of
\mathsf{ML}
and
\mathsf{GML}
with the constructors in
\mathsf{e}\subseteq\{\mathsf{i},\mathsf{u},\mathsf{n}\}
, where
\mathsf{i}
stands for inverse modalities,
\mathsf{u}
the universal modality, and
\mathsf{n}
nominals.

\mathsf{GML}/\mathsf{ML}

Given an
L
-formula
\varphi
, the proofs of the complexity results for
L/L^{\prime}
-definability and uniform separation in Table
I
construct an
L^{\prime}
-formula
\varphi^{\prime}
that is a definition/uniform separator for
\varphi
iff one exists and give polytime reductions to
L
-validity (sometimes also over finite structures). The surprisingly simple construction is inspired by approaches to
\mathsf{FO}/\mathsf{ML}
and
\mathsf{MSO}/\mu\mathsf{ML}
-definability via
\omega
-expansions
[
31
,
32
]
.
For
\mathsf{C^{2}}
and nominal-free modal logics, the formula
\varphi^{\prime}
is constructed in polytime (if given as a DAG); if nominals are present,
\varphi^{\prime}
is of exponential size and more work is needed to prove a polytime reduction to (finite)
L
-validity. We also show
tight complexity bounds for other pairs not mentioned in Table
I
explicitly, say
\mathsf{C^{2}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}
and
\mathsf{C^{2}}/\mathsf{ML}
. As

\mathsf{C^{2}}
is exponentially more succinct than
\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}
(even for DAG-representation), to obtain polytime reductions to validity, we work with a succinct version of
\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}
inspired by the presentation of
\mathsf{FO^{2}}
as a modal logic
[
33
]
.

The proofs for (Craig)
L/L^{\prime}
-separation follow a completely different approach and use a basic characterisation of non-separation of
\varphi
and
\psi
in
L^{\prime}
via the existence of
L^{\prime}
-bisimilar models satisfying
\varphi
and
\psi
, for an appropriate notion of
L^{\prime}
-bisimulation. In this case, our complexity results hold for the pairs
L/L^{\prime}
in Table
I
and also all weakenings of
L^{\prime}
mentioned in Table
I
(except for
L=\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}}
and
L^{\prime}
without inverse modalities, where undecidability is open). The undecidability results are established by a reduction of the non-halting problem for Minsky machines, in which, remarkably, a single nominal fixing the root of a model is enough to obtain the undecidability for
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}}
.
The technically most demanding proof is the
2ExpTime
upper bound for
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}
-separation, which uses a mosaic technique to show that if one cannot fix the root of models using a nominal, the construction of bisimilar models can be regularised. The difference between
coNExpTime
and
2ExpTime
is due to the presence/absence of the universal modality without which one can only look polynomially-deep into the bisimilar models.

Related Results.

For a decidable
\mathsf{FO}
-fragment
L
and
L^{\prime}\in\{\mathsf{ML},

\mathsf{ML\!^{i}}\}
, the decidability of
L/L^{\prime}
-definability follows from Otto’s result that if an
\mathsf{FO}
-formula
\varphi(x)
has an
L^{\prime}
-definition, then it has an
L^{\prime}
-definition of modal depth
\leq 2^{n}-1
, where
n
is the quantifier rank of
\varphi(x)

[
34
]
. No elementary complexity bound is obtained using this approach. A
2ExpTime
-upper bound for
\mathsf{GML\!^{u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{u}}
-definability is shown in
[
35
]
in the context of DL TBoxes.
Independently from this paper, and using different methods,
2ExpTime
-completeness of
\mathsf{GML}/\mathsf{ML}
-separation is shown in
[
36
]
, even for
\mathsf{GML}
with fixpoint-operators.
For further discussion of separation and definability problems and their relationship to Craig interpolation, we refer the reader to
[
37
]
.

The paper is organised as follows. All relevant logics and their semantics are defined in Sec.
II
. Model-theoretic characterisations of separation in terms of bisimulations are given in Sec.
III
, which also shows polytime reductions between some of our decision problems. The undecidability results for separation are proved in Sec.
IV
and the decidability ones in Secs.
V
,
VI
. Definability is considered in Sec.
VII
and uniform separation in Sec.
VIII
. Finally, Sec.
IX
discusses future work.

All omitted proofs can be found in the appendix.

II
Logics

First, we define the syntax and semantics of logics in Table
I
. Let
\sigma
be a (possibly infinite)
signature
of unary and binary predicate symbols and constants, let
\mathsf{FO}(\sigma)
denote the set of first-order formulas with equality over
\sigma
, and let
var
be a set comprising two individual variables. Then

\mathsf{FO^{2}}(\sigma)
,

the
two-variable fragment of
\mathsf{FO}(\sigma)
, is the set of formulas that are built from atoms of the form
\top(x)
,
A(x)
,
R(x,y)
, and
x=y
with unary
A\in\sigma
, binary
R\in\sigma
, and
x,y\in\textit{var}
, using the Boolean connectives
\wedge
and
\neg
and quantifier
\exists x
with
x\in\textit{var}
(other Boolean connectives and
\forall x
are regarded as standard abbreviations);

\mathsf{FO^{2}_{\neq}}(\sigma)

is the
equality-free fragment of
\mathsf{FO^{2}}(\sigma)
;

\mathsf{C^{2}}(\sigma)
,

the
two-variable fragment of
\mathsf{FO}(\sigma)
with counting
, extends
\mathsf{FO^{2}}(\sigma)
with the counting quantifiers
\exists^{\geq k}x
, for
k\geq 1
and
x\in\textit{var}
(other counting quantifiers
\exists^{=k}x
,
\exists^{<k}x
,
\exists^{>k}x
, etc. are regarded as abbreviations).

Since constants,
c
, can be defined in
\mathsf{FO^{2}}
using fresh unary predicates
C
as
\exists x\,\big(C(x)\land\forall y\,(C(y)\to x=y)\big)
, when dealing with
\mathsf{FO^{2}}(\sigma)
and
\mathsf{C^{2}}(\sigma)
, we assume that
\sigma
is constant-free.

We also consider a few fragments of
\mathsf{FO^{2}}
and
\mathsf{C^{2}}
corresponding to standard propositional (graded) modal logics. In this context, each unary
A\in\sigma
is treated as a
propositional variable
and each binary
R\in\sigma
gives rise to
graded modal operators

\Diamond\!^{\geq k}_{R}
, for
k\geq 1
.

\mathsf{ML}(\sigma)
,

the basic
modal logic
over
\sigma
, is defined as the set of formulas given by the following grammar with
A,R\in\sigma
:

\varphi\ :=\ \top\ \mid\ A\ \mid\ \neg\varphi\ \mid\ \varphi\land\varphi^{\prime}\ \mid\ \Diamond\!^{\geq 1}_{R}\varphi
. Instead of
\Diamond\!^{\geq 1}_{R}\varphi
, we usually write
\Diamond_{R}\varphi
.

\mathsf{GML}(\sigma)
,

the
graded modal logic
over
\sigma
, extends
\mathsf{ML}(\sigma)
with graded modal operators
\Diamond\!^{\geq k}_{R}
, for any
k\geq 1
and
R\in\sigma
.

The popular extensions of
\mathsf{ML}
and
\mathsf{GML}
with the
inverse
modal operators,
universal modality
, and/or
nominals
are denoted by
\mathsf{ML}\!^{\mathsf{e}}(\sigma)
and
\mathsf{GML}\!^{\mathsf{e}}(\sigma)
, where
\mathsf{e}\subseteq\{\mathsf{i},\mathsf{u},\mathsf{n}\}
and

\mathsf{i}

indicates that whenever
\Diamond\!^{\geq k}_{R}
is admitted in the logic in question, then its
inverse

\Diamond\!^{\geq k}_{R^{-}}
is also admitted;

\mathsf{u}

indicates admittance of the
universal diamond

\Diamondblack\varphi
;

\mathsf{n}

indicates that
\sigma
may contain individual constants
c
, which give rise to atomic formulas
N_{c}
, known as
nominals
.

Thus,
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}}
is our strongest graded modal logic. We see it as a fragment of
\mathsf{C^{2}}
through the lens of the
standard translation

\cdot^{\ast}
, which rephrases any
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}}
-formula
\varphi
as the
\mathsf{C^{2}}
-formula
\varphi^{\ast}_{x}
with one free variable
x\in\textit{var}
defined inductively by taking

\displaystyle\top^{\ast}_{x}=\top(x),\ \ A^{\ast}_{x}=A(x),\ \ {(N_{c})}_{x}^{\ast}=(x=c),\ \ (\neg\varphi)_{x}^{\ast}=\neg\varphi_{x}^{\ast},

\displaystyle(\varphi\land\psi)_{x}^{\ast}=\varphi_{x}^{\ast}\land\psi_{x}^{\ast},\ \ (\Diamondblack\varphi)_{x}^{\ast}=\top(x)\land\exists\bar{x}\,\varphi_{\bar{x}}^{\ast},

\displaystyle(\Diamond^{\geq k}_{R}\varphi)_{x}^{\ast}=\exists^{\geq k}\bar{x}\,(R(x,\bar{x})\land\varphi_{\bar{x}}^{\ast}),\,(\Diamond^{\geq k}_{R^{-}}\varphi)_{x}^{\ast}=\exists^{\geq k}\bar{x}\,(R(\bar{x},x)\land\varphi_{\bar{x}}^{\ast})

where
\bar{x}=y
,
\bar{y}=x
and
\{x,y\}=\textit{var}
.

The
signature
of
\varphi
is the set
\mathit{sig}(\varphi)
of predicate and constant symbols in
\varphi
.
The set
\textit{sub}(\varphi)
comprises all subformulas of
\varphi
together with their negations;
\textit{sub}(\varphi,\psi)=\textit{sub}(\varphi)\cup\textit{sub}(\psi)
. We assume that formulas are given as DAGs and the parameter
k
in
\exists^{\geq k}
and
\Diamond\!^{\geq k}_{R}
is encoded in binary, in which case the
size
of
\varphi
is bounded by
|\varphi|=|\textit{sub}(\varphi)|\cdot\log k^{\bullet}
, where
k^{\bullet}
is the largest parameter in
\varphi
. Our complexity results remain the same if we adopt the unary encoding of parameters and define the size of
\varphi
as the number of occurrences of symbols in it.

A
\sigma
-
structure
takes the form
\mathfrak{A}=({\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}),(Q^{\mathfrak{A}})_{Q\in\sigma},(c^{\mathfrak{A}})_{c\in\sigma})
with a
domain

{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})\neq\emptyset
, relations
Q^{\mathfrak{A}}
on
{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})
of the same arity as
Q\in\sigma
, and
c^{\mathfrak{A}}\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})
, for constants
c\in\sigma
.
We write
\varphi\models\psi
to say that
\varphi

entails

\psi
in the sense that if
\varphi
is true in some structure
\mathfrak{A}
under some assignment of elements of
{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})
to the variables in
var
, then
\psi
is also true in
\mathfrak{A}
under the same assignment. If
\varphi\models\psi
and
\psi\models\varphi
, we write
\varphi\equiv\psi
, calling
\varphi
and
\psi

equivalent
.

The complexities of the satisfiability problems for the logics above are as follows (see
[
38
,
12
,
39
]
and references therein):

–

\mathsf{FO^{2}}
,
\mathsf{C^{2}}
,
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}}
, and
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n}}
are
NExpTime
-complete;

–

\mathsf{ML\!^{u}}
,
\mathsf{GML\!^{u}}
,
\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}
,
\mathsf{ML\!^{n,u}}
,
\mathsf{GML\!^{n,u}}
,
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,u}}
and
\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}
are
ExpTime
-complete;

–

\mathsf{ML}
,
\mathsf{GML}
,
\mathsf{ML\!^{i}}
,
\mathsf{GML\!^{i}}
,
\mathsf{ML\!^{n}}
,
\mathsf{GML\!^{n}}
are
PSpace
-complete.

III
Separation and Bisimulation

We now introduce the basic semantic instruments that are required for determining the decidability and complexity of the decision problems formulated in Sec.
I
for the pairs
L/L^{\prime}
of logics such that

–

L
is any logic with counting defined in Sec.
II
;

–

L^{\prime}\subseteq L
is any logic without counting from Sec.
II
.

From now on, we assume that a signature
\sigma
is fixed, that
L
and
L^{\prime}
range over the logics with and, respectively, without counting defined in Sec.
II
, and that
L\supseteq L^{\prime}
in every pair
L/L^{\prime}
. (As
\sigma
in
\mathsf{C^{2}}(\sigma)
is assumed to be constant-free, we do not consider pairs such as
\mathsf{C^{2}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{n}}
.) To simplify presentation and since most of our logics are modal, here we only consider formulas
\varphi(x)
with one free variable
x\in\textit{var}
.

A
pointed structure
is a pair
\mathfrak{A},a
with
a\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})
. We write
\mathfrak{A}\models\varphi(a)
if
a
satisfies
\varphi(x)
in
\mathfrak{A}
. Given
\varrho\subseteq\sigma
and pointed
\sigma
-structures
\mathfrak{A},a
and
\mathfrak{B},b
, we write
\mathfrak{A},a\equiv_{L(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},b
and call
\mathfrak{A},a
and
\mathfrak{B},b

L(\varrho)
-equivalent
in case
\mathfrak{A}\models\varphi(a)
iff
\mathfrak{B}\models\varphi(b)
, for all
L(\varrho)
-formulas
\varphi(x)
.

A non-empty binary relation
\boldsymbol{\beta}\subseteq{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})\times{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{B})
is an
\mathsf{FO^{2}}(\varrho)
-bisimulation between
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}

if
\boldsymbol{\beta}
is
global
—that is,
{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})\subseteq\{a\mid(a,b)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}\}
and
{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{B})\subseteq\{b\mid(a,b)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}\}
—and for
every
(a,b)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
, the following conditions are satisfied:

–

for every
a^{\prime}\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})
, there is a
b^{\prime}\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{B})
such that
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
(a,a^{\prime})\mapsto(b,b^{\prime})
is a partial
\varrho
-isomorphism between
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
;

–

for every
b^{\prime}\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{B})
, there is an
a^{\prime}\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})
such that
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
(a,a^{\prime})\mapsto(b,b^{\prime})
is a partial
\varrho
-isomorphism between
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
.

We write
\mathfrak{A},a\sim_{\mathsf{FO^{2}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},b
if

a\mapsto b
is a partial
\varrho
-isomorphism between
\mathfrak{A}
and

\mathfrak{B}
and there is an
\mathsf{FO^{2}}(\varrho)
-bisimulation
\boldsymbol{\beta}
between
\mathfrak{A}
and

\mathfrak{B}
such that
(a,b)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
.

A non-empty
\boldsymbol{\beta}\subseteq{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})\times{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{B})
is an
\mathsf{ML}(\varrho)
-bisimulation between
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
if the following conditions hold:

–

if
(a,b)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
, then
a\in A^{\mathfrak{A}}
iff
b\in A^{\mathfrak{B}}
for all
A\in\varrho
;

–

if
(a,b)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
(a,a^{\prime})\in R^{\mathfrak{A}}
, for
R\in\varrho
, then there exists
b^{\prime}\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{B})
such that
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
(b,b^{\prime})\in R^{\mathfrak{B}}
;

–

if
(a,b)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
(b,b^{\prime})\in R^{\mathfrak{B}}
, for
R\in\varrho
, then there exists
a^{\prime}\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})
such that
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
(a,a^{\prime})\in R^{\mathfrak{A}}
.

Further, an
\mathsf{ML}(\varrho)
-bisimulation
\boldsymbol{\beta}
between
\mathfrak{A}
,
\mathfrak{B}
is called an

\mathsf{ML\!^{i}}(\varrho)
-bisimulation

if the second and third conditions also hold for
R=P^{-}
with
P\in\varrho
;

\mathsf{ML\!^{n}}(\varrho)
-bisimulation

if
(c^{\mathfrak{A}},c^{\mathfrak{B}})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
, for each constant
c\in\varrho
;

\mathsf{ML\!^{u}}(\varrho)
-bisimulation

if it is global.

\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}(\varrho)
-,
\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}(\varrho)
-, etc. bisimulations are those
\boldsymbol{\beta}
that satisfy all of the conditions for the respective superscripts.
For a modal logic
L^{\prime}
, we write
\mathfrak{A},a\sim_{L^{\prime}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},b
if there is an
L^{\prime}(\varrho)
-bisimulation
\boldsymbol{\beta}
between
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
such that
(a,b)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
.

The following characterisation is well-known; see, e.g.,
[
40
,
41
,
12
]
(see also
[
42
]
for a discussion of
\omega
-saturated structures).

Lemma 3
.

For any
L^{\prime}
,
\varrho\subseteq\sigma
, and any pointed
\sigma
-structures
\mathfrak{A},a
and
\mathfrak{B},b
,

\mathfrak{A},a\sim_{L^{\prime}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},b\quad\text{ implies }\quad\mathfrak{A},a\equiv_{L^{\prime}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},b

and, conversely, if structures
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
are
\omega
-saturated, then

\mathfrak{A},a\equiv_{L^{\prime}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},b\quad\text{ implies }\quad\mathfrak{A},a\sim_{L^{\prime}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},b.

An
L^{\prime}(\varrho)
-
separator
for
L
-formulas
\varphi(x)
and
\psi(x)
is any
L^{\prime}(\varrho)
-formula
\chi(x)
such that
\varphi(x)\models\chi(x)
and
\chi(x)\models\neg\psi(x)
.
The next criterion is similar to Robinson’s joint consistency theorem
[
43
]
:

Lemma 4
.

For any pair
L/L^{\prime}
, any
L
-formulas
\varphi(x)
,
\psi(x)
and any
\varrho\subseteq\sigma
, the following conditions are equivalent
:

–

\varphi(x)
and
\psi(x)
do not have an
L^{\prime}(\varrho)
-separator
;

–

there are pointed
\sigma
-structures
\mathfrak{A},a
and
\mathfrak{B},b
such that

\mathfrak{A}\models\varphi(a),\quad\mathfrak{B}\models\psi(b),\quad\mathfrak{A},a\sim_{L^{\prime}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},b.

Proof.

The proof is in line with the characterisations of Craig interpolant existence in
[
44
,
29
]
.

\dashv

We next observe a few reductions between the decision problems from Sec.
I
. The next lemma requires the notion of
unary Craig
L/L^{\prime}
-separation
, which lies between
L/L^{\prime}
-separation and Craig
L/L^{\prime}
-separation: an
L^{\prime}
-separator
\chi
for
\varphi
and
\psi
is called a
unary Craig
L/L^{\prime}
-separator
if all unary
A\in\mathit{sig}(\chi)
occur in both
\varphi
and
\psi
(non-unary predicates may be arbitrary). In modal logic, where unary predicates are regarded as propositional variables, this notion has actually been frequently considered for Craig interpolation when one does not want to restrict the use of modal operators in interpolants. Also, unary Craig separation makes our undecidability and lower complexity bound proofs more transparent.

Lemma 5
.

For any pair
L/L^{\prime}
,

–

L/L^{\prime}
-separation is polytime reducible to Craig
L/L^{\prime}
-separation and to unary Craig
L/L^{\prime}
-separation
;

–

unary Craig
L/L^{\prime}
-separation is polytime reducible to
L/L^{\prime}
-separation.

While the former reduction is easy, the proof of the latter relies on Lemma
4
. Essentially, one replaces non-shared unary predicates in
\varphi
and
\psi
by compound counting formulas over shared symbols. Note also that, for all
L/L^{\prime}
, we prove exactly the same (mostly tight) complexity bounds for plain separation and Craig separation. However, we could not find a ‘black-box’ polytime reduction of Craig separation to separation.

Finally, we lift the well-established link between Craig interpolation and (projective) Beth definability
[
30
,
45
]
to a link between Craig
L/L^{\prime}
-separation and an appropriate notion of
L/L^{\prime}
-definability.

relative
L/L^{\prime}
-definability:

given a signature
\varrho
and two
L
-formulas
\varphi
and
\psi
, decide whether there exists an
explicit
L^{\prime}(\varrho)
-definition of
\psi
modulo

\varphi
, i.e., an
L^{\prime}(\varrho)
-formula
\delta
such that
\varphi\models\delta\leftrightarrow\psi
.

Lemma 6
.

For any pair
L/L^{\prime}
, Craig
L/L^{\prime}
-separation and relative
L/L^{\prime}
-definability are polytime reducible to each other.

Using a standard trick from, e.g.,
[
46
]
, the proof shows how the construction of separators can be used to construct definitions and the other way round.

IV
Separation in
\mathsf{C^{2}}
and
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}}
is Undecidable

Our first result is negative. It shows that separation of
\mathsf{C^{2}}
- and
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}}
-formulas by means of (nearly all) smaller counting-free languages in Table
I
is undecidable:

Theorem 7
.

The
L/L^{\prime}
-separation and Craig
L/L^{\prime}
-separation problems are RE-complete
(
and so undecidable
)
for

–

L=\mathsf{C^{2}}
and any counting-free
L^{\prime}
in Table
I
, and also for

–

L=\{\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}},\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n}}\}
and any
L^{\prime}=\mathsf{ML}\!^{\mathsf{e}}
in Table
I
such that
\mathsf{i}\in\mathsf{e}\subseteq\{\mathsf{i},\mathsf{n},\mathsf{u}\}
.

We first explain the idea of the proof for
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}
. We encode the non-halting problem for Minsky (aka counter or register) machines in terms of Craig-separator non-existence. A
Minsky machine
M
with two registers

\mathit{reg}_{0}
,
\mathit{reg}_{1}
(2RM, for short) is given by a set
Q=\{q_{0},\dots,q_{m}\}
of states with initial
q_{0}
and final
q_{m}
, and instructions
I_{i}
,
i<m
, that can be of two forms:
I_{i}=+(\ell,q_{j})
and
I_{i}=-(\ell,q_{j},q_{l})
, for
\ell\in\{0,1\}
. The former says that, when in state
q_{i}
,
M
increments register
\mathit{reg}_{\ell}
by one and moves to state
q_{j}
. The latter means: in state
q_{i}
, if
\mathit{reg}_{\ell}>0
,
M
decrements
\mathit{reg}_{\ell}
by one and moves to state
q_{l}
; if
\mathit{reg}_{\ell}=0
,
M
moves to state
q_{j}
.

The
halting problem
is to decide, given a 2RM
M
, whether starting in
q_{0}
with
\mathit{reg}_{0}=\mathit{reg}_{1}=0
,
M
will reach the final state
q_{m}
. This problem is RE-complete
[
47
]
. We assume without loss of generality that
q_{0}
does not occur in
M
’s instructions and that
I_{0}
is a decrement instruction.

Given such an
M
, we define two
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}}
-formulas
\varphi_{M}
,
\psi_{M}
. Intuitively,
\varphi_{M}
describes
M
’s successive states,
\psi_{M}
captures the content of the registers, and the bisimulation from the
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}
-separation criterion (Lemma
4
) makes the two sequences match, thereby simulating the computation of
M
.
The shared signature,
\varrho
, of
\varphi_{M}
and
\psi_{M}
comprises propositional variables
q_{i}\in Q
and
e_{\ell}
,
\ell=0,1
, saying that
\mathit{reg}_{\ell}=0
, a binary predicate
R
for computation steps, and a constant
c
for the starting point of the computation. Additionally,
\psi_{M}
uses (non-shared) propositional variables
r_{0}
and
r_{1}\equiv\neg r_{0}
for encoding the registers and auxiliary
u
whose purpose is explained later.

To illustrate, suppose
M
contains (among others) instructions
I_{0}=-(0,q_{1},q_{1})
,
I_{1}=+(0,q_{4})
and
I_{4}=-(0,q_{2},q_{4})
. The intended structures
\mathfrak{A},a\models\varphi_{M}
and
\mathfrak{B},b\models\psi_{M}
look like in the picture below. The formula
\varphi_{M}
generates an infinite
R
-chain

\mathfrak{A},a
q_{0}
N_{c},e_{0},e_{1}
q_{1}
q_{4}
q_{4}
e_{0}
q_{2}
\vdots
R
R
R
R
\mathfrak{B},b
q_{0},r_{1}
N_{c},e_{0},e_{1}
q_{1},r_{0}
u
q_{1},r_{1}
u
q_{4},r_{0}
u
q_{4},r_{0}
q_{4},r_{1}
u
q_{4},r_{1}
e_{0},u
q_{2},r_{1}
u
q_{4},r_{0}
e_{0},u
q_{2},r_{0}
u
R
R
R
R
R
R
R
R
R
R
\vdots
\vdots

starting from
a
, each of whose points satisfies exactly one of the
q_{i}\in Q
. The order of these
q_{i}
follows the order of states in the computation of
M
, without ever reaching
q_{m}
. The variables
e_{\ell}
are supposed to indicate the computation steps where the current instruction is to decrement
\mathit{reg}_{\ell}=0
(their behaviour depends on both
\varphi_{M}
and
\psi_{M}
). Syntactically,

\displaystyle\varphi_{M}=~

\displaystyle N_{c}\wedge q_{0}\land\blacksquare\bigvee_{i\leq m}q_{i}\land\blacksquare\bigwedge_{i\leq m}(q_{i}\rightarrow\bigwedge_{i<j\leq m}\neg q_{j})\land{}

\displaystyle\square_{R^{-}}\bot\land\blacksquare\Diamond\!^{=1}_{R}\top\land\blacksquare(\neg N_{c}\rightarrow\Diamond\!^{=1}_{R^{-}}\top)\land\blacksquare\neg q_{m}\land{}

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{I_{i}=+(\ell,q_{j})}\blacksquare(q_{i}\to\square_{R}q_{j})\land{}

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{I_{i}=-(\ell,q_{j},q_{k})}\hskip-8.53581pt\blacksquare((q_{i}\wedge e_{\ell})\rightarrow\square_{R}q_{j})\land\blacksquare((q_{i}\wedge\neg e_{\ell})\rightarrow\square_{R}q_{k}).

The formula
\psi_{M}
simulates the behaviour of the registers
\mathit{reg}_{\ell}
by generating an
R
-directed graph with root
b
whose nodes of
depth

k\geq 0
(that is, located at distance
k
from
b
) represent the value of
\mathit{reg}_{\ell}
at step
k
of
M
’s computation in the sense that this value is
n
iff the number of depth
k
nodes that make
r_{\ell}
true is
2^{n}
. We explain the intuition behind
\psi_{M}
by dividing it into four groups of conjuncts. The first one comprises the same conjuncts as the first line of the definition of
\varphi_{M}
.

The second group consists of the conjuncts

\displaystyle e_{0}\land e_{1}\land\Diamond\!_{R}^{=2}\top\land\Diamond_{R}(u\wedge r_{0})\wedge\Diamond_{R}(u\wedge r_{1})\land{}

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{\ell=0,1}\blacksquare\big[r_{\ell}\rightarrow\big(\square_{R}r_{\ell}\wedge\square_{R^{-1}}(r_{\ell}\vee N_{c})\big)\big]\land{}

\displaystyle\blacksquare\big((u\wedge\neg N_{c})\rightarrow(\Diamond\!_{R}^{=1}u\wedge\Diamond\!_{R^{-1}}^{=1}u)\big).

It generates one
r_{0}
-node and one
r_{1}
-node of depth 1, makes sure that all
R
-successors and all non-root
R
-predecessors of an
r_{\ell}
-node are also
r_{\ell}
-nodes, and singles out one branch of
r_{\ell}
-nodes, for each
\ell=0,1
, by making the auxiliary variable
u
true at its nodes. Thus, for each
k\geq 1
,
u
is true at exactly one
r_{\ell}
-node of depth
k
. This fact will be used to handle the variables
e_{\ell}
that are needed for checking whether
\mathit{reg}_{\ell}=0
.

The third group simulates the instructions using the following conjuncts (see the picture above for an illustration):

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{I_{i}=+(\ell,q_{j})}\hskip-5.69054pt\blacksquare\big[\big((q_{i}\wedge r_{\ell})\rightarrow(\Diamond\!_{R}^{=2}\top\wedge\square_{R}\Diamond\!_{R^{-1}}^{=1}\top)\big)\land{}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\big((q_{i}\wedge\neg r_{\ell})\rightarrow(\Diamond\!_{R}^{=1}\top\wedge\square_{R}\Diamond\!_{R^{-1}}^{=1}\top)\big)\big]\land{}

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq 0\\
I_{i}=-(\ell,q_{j},q_{l})\end{subarray}}\hskip-8.53581pt\blacksquare\big[\big((q_{i}\wedge r_{\ell}\wedge e_{\ell})\rightarrow(\Diamond\!_{R}^{=1}\top\wedge\square_{R}\Diamond\!_{R^{-1}}^{=1}\top)\big)\land{}

\displaystyle\hskip 65.44142pt\big((q_{i}\wedge r_{\ell}\wedge\neg e_{\ell})\rightarrow(\Diamond\!_{R}^{=1}\top\wedge\square_{R}\Diamond\!_{R^{-1}}^{=2}\top)\big)\land{}

\displaystyle\hskip 93.89418pt\big((q_{i}\wedge\neg r_{\ell})\rightarrow(\Diamond\!_{R}^{=1}\top\wedge\square_{R}\Diamond\!_{R^{-1}}^{=1}\top)\big)\big].

For the increment instructions
I_{i}=+(\ell,q_{j})
, each node of depth
k
making
q_{i}
and
r_{\ell}
true has two
R
-successors, each of which has one
R
-predecessor. Each
\neg r_{\ell}
-node of depth
k
has one
R
-successor. This doubles the number of
r_{\ell}
-nodes of depth
k
while keeping the number of
\neg r_{\ell}
-nodes unchanged as required.
For the decrement instructions
I_{i}=-(\ell,q_{j},q_{l})
, we use the variables
e_{\ell}
to check if
\mathit{reg}_{\ell}=0
. If this is the case, each
r_{\ell}
-node of depth
k
has one
R
-successor that has one
R
-predecessor, which keeps
\mathit{reg}_{\ell}=0
at step
k+1
. Otherwise, each
r_{\ell}
-node of depth
k
has a single
R
-successor that has two
R
-predecessors, which halves the number of such nodes.
The nodes of depth
k
in the other register (i.e., those satisfying
\neg r_{\ell}
) have exactly one
R
-successor that has exactly one
R
-predecessor, thereby maintaining the value of the register.
Decrement instruction
I_{0}
is treated separately by the second group that creates registers
\mathit{reg}_{\ell}=0
,
\ell=0,1
, at step (depth) 1.

Finally, we require the conjunct
\blacksquare\big((e_{\ell}\land r_{\ell})\rightarrow u\big)
to make sure that, if
\mathfrak{A}\models\varphi_{M}(a)
,
\mathfrak{B}\models\psi_{M}(b)
, and
\mathfrak{A},a\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},b
, then
e_{\ell}
is true at nodes representing the computation steps with a decrement instruction and
\mathit{reg}_{\ell}=0
.

It is straightforward to see that, for a non-halting
M
, the intended pointed structures
\mathfrak{A},a
and
\mathfrak{B},b
described above are such that
\mathfrak{A}\models\varphi_{M}(a)
,
\mathfrak{B}\models\psi_{M}(b)
, and
\mathfrak{A},a\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},b
, with a
\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}(\varrho)
-bisimulation
\boldsymbol{\beta}
obtained by taking
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
iff
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
are at the same distance
k<\omega
from
a
in
\mathfrak{A}
and, respectively,
b
in
\mathfrak{B}
. It follows that
\varphi_{M}
and
\psi_{M}
are not
\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}(\varrho)
-separable.
Conversely, we prove in the appendix that if there are
\mathfrak{A},a
and
\mathfrak{B},b
with
\mathfrak{A}\models\varphi_{M}(a)
,
\mathfrak{B}\models\psi_{M}(b)
, and
\mathfrak{A},a\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},b
, then
M
is non-halting. In fact, the proof shows that, in this case,
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
must contain the intended structures as bisimilar sub-structures.

By Lemma
4
, it follows that Craig
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}
-separation is RE-complete and undecidable. This is also true of unary Craig
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}
-separation because
\varphi_{M}
and
\psi_{M}
have the same binary predicates. And using the second claim of Lemma
5
, we obtain that
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}
-separation is RE-complete.
We can adapt the construction above to prove Theorem
7
for
\Diamondblack
- and
\blacksquare
-free
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n}}
using the
spypoint
technique
[
39
]
; see the appendix for details. To deal with the nominal-free
L^{\prime}
, we can simulate
N_{c}
by adding to
\varphi_{M}
the conjunct
N_{c}\leftrightarrow p
with a fresh propositional variable
p
and replace
N_{c}
in
\psi_{M}
with
p
, making it shared in
\varphi_{M}
and
\psi_{M}
.

Finally, the first claim of Theorem
7
is proved similarly to the graded modal case, with some minor modifications required to take care of
\mathsf{FO^{2}}(\varrho)
-bisimulations, which are more restricted than
\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}(\varrho)
-bisimulations; see the appendix.

The next two sections establish the positive tight complexity results for (Craig) separation in Table
I
, starting with the technically simpler case of graded modal logics without inverses.

V
Separation in Fragments of
\mathsf{GML\!^{n,u}}

The aim of this section is to prove the following:

Theorem 8
.

Separation and Craig-separation are

2\textsc{ExpTime}
-complete

for the pairs
\mathsf{GML\!^{n,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{n,u}}
,
\mathsf{GML\!^{n,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{n}}
,
\mathsf{GML\!^{n,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{u}}
,
\mathsf{GML\!^{u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{u}}
, and
\mathsf{GML\!^{u}}/\mathsf{ML}
;

coNExpTime
-complete

for
\mathsf{GML\!^{n}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{n}}
and
\mathsf{GML}/\mathsf{ML}
.

We begin by establishing the upper bounds. By Lemma
5
, it suffices to consider Craig separation. Let
\varphi_{1}
,
\varphi_{2}
be
\mathsf{GML\!^{n,u}}
-formulas,
\sigma=\mathit{sig}(\varphi_{1})\cup\mathit{sig}(\varphi_{2})
and
\varrho=\mathit{sig}(\varphi_{1})\cap\mathit{sig}(\varphi_{2})
.
A
type
(
for
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
) is any set
\mathfrak{t}\subseteq\textit{sub}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
, for which there is a pointed
\sigma
-structure
\mathfrak{A},a
such that
\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}(\mathfrak{A},a)
, where

\mathfrak{t}(\mathfrak{A},a)=\{\chi\in\textit{sub}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\mid\mathfrak{A}\models\chi(a)\}

is the
type

realised by
a
in
\mathfrak{A}
. Denote by
\boldsymbol{T}
the set of all such types. Clearly,
|\boldsymbol{T}|\leq 2^{|\textit{sub}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})|}
and
\boldsymbol{T}
can be computed in time exponential in
|\varphi_{1}|+|\varphi_{2}|
.
Types
\mathfrak{t}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{t}_{2}
are called
\Diamondblack
-equivalent

in case
\Diamondblack\chi\in\mathfrak{t}_{1}
iff
\Diamondblack\chi\in\mathfrak{t}_{2}
, for all
\Diamondblack\chi\in\textit{sub}(\varphi,\psi)
. Note that the types realised in the same structure are
\Diamondblack
-equivalent to each other.

We check the existence of
\sigma
-structures
\mathfrak{A}_{1},a_{1}
and
\mathfrak{A},a_{2}
such that
\mathfrak{A}_{i}\models\varphi_{i}(a_{i})
,
i=1,2
, and
\mathfrak{A}_{1},a_{1}\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{n,u}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{A}_{2},a_{2}
using a mosaic-elimination procedure, a generalisation of the standard type-elimination procedure for satisfiability-checking; see, e.g.,
[
48
, p. 72]
.

A
mosaic
(
for
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
) is a pair
M=(M_{1},M_{2})
of non-empty sets
M_{1},M_{2}
of types satisfying the following conditions:

–

A\in\mathfrak{t}
iff
A\in\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
, for all
A\in\varrho
and
\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in M_{1}\cup M_{2}
;

–

N_{c}\in\mathfrak{t}
iff
N_{c}\in\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
, for all
c\in\varrho
and
\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in M_{1}\cup M_{2}
;

–

if
\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in M_{i}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
,
N_{c}\in\mathfrak{t}\cap\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
,
c\in\sigma
, then
\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
;

–

all types in each
M_{i}
are
\Diamondblack
-equivalent to each other.

We say that
N_{c}

occurs
in
M
if
N_{c}\in\mathfrak{t}\in M_{i}
, for some
\mathfrak{t}
and
i\in\{1,2\}
.
As follows from the second and third items, if
N_{c}
occurs in
M
, for some
c\in\varrho
, then
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are singletons.

There are at most doubly exponentially many mosaics, and they can be computed in double exponential time. Let
f
be the function defined by taking
f(M)=1
if
M
contains a type with a nominal
and
f(M)=k^{\bullet}
otherwise, where
k^{\bullet}
is the maximal
k
such that
\Diamond_{R}^{\geq k}
occurs in
\varphi_{1}
or
\varphi_{2}
, for some
R
.

The definition of mosaic above is an abstraction from the following semantical notion. Given structures
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{A}_{2}
, the

mosaic defined by
a\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{i})
in
\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
,
is the pair
M(\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2},a)=(M_{1}(\mathfrak{A}_{1},a),M_{2}(\mathfrak{A}_{2},a))
, where, for
j\in\{1,2\}
,

M_{j}(\mathfrak{A}_{j},a)=\{\mathfrak{t}(\mathfrak{A}_{j},b)\mid b\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{j}),\ \mathfrak{A}_{i},a\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{n,u}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{A}_{j},b\}.

Clearly,
M(\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2},a)
is a mosaic and
M(\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2},a)=M(\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2},a^{\prime})
if
a\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{n,u}}(\varrho)}a^{\prime}
, for any
a,a^{\prime}\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{1})\cup{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{2})
.

We aim to build the required structures
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
,
i=1,2
, and bisimulation
\boldsymbol{\beta}
between them from copies of suitable pairs
(\mathfrak{t},M)
with a mosaic
M=(M_{1},M_{2})
and a type
\mathfrak{t}\in M_{i}
, for which we, roughly, aim to set
(\mathfrak{t}_{1},M)\boldsymbol{\beta}(\mathfrak{t}_{2},M)
.
To satisfy the modalities
\Diamond_{R}^{\geq k}\chi
in the
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
, we require the following definition.
Let
M=(M_{1},M_{2})
be a mosaic,
\mathcal{M}
a set of mosaics, and some type in
M_{1}\cup M_{2}
contain
\Diamond_{R}^{\geq k}\chi
. We call
\mathcal{M}
an
R
-witness for
M
if there exists a relation
R^{M,\mathcal{M}}\subseteq(\Delta_{1}\times\Gamma_{1})\cup(\Delta_{2}\times\Gamma_{2})
with

\displaystyle\Delta_{i}=\{(\mathfrak{t},M)\mid\mathfrak{t}\in M_{i}\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i}=\{(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},M^{\prime},j)\mid\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in M_{i}^{\prime},\ M^{\prime}=(M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{M},\ 1\leq j\leq f(M^{\prime})\}

satisfying the following for
\Delta=\Delta_{1}\cup\Delta_{2}
and
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}
:

(
\Diamondblack
-harmony)

all types in
M_{i}
and
M_{i}^{\prime}
, for any
M^{\prime}=(M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{M}
, are
\Diamondblack
-equivalent to each other,
i=1,2
;

(witness)

for any
\Diamond^{\geq k}_{R}\chi\in\textit{sub}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
and
(\mathfrak{t},M)\in\Delta
, we have

\mbox{}\hskip-19.91692pt\Diamond^{\geq k}_{R}\chi\in\mathfrak{t}\text{ iff }\big|\{(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},M^{\prime},j)\in\Gamma\mid\chi\in\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},(\mathfrak{t},M)R^{M,\mathcal{M}}(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},M^{\prime},j)\}\big|\geq k;

(bisim)

if
R\in\varrho
,
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
, and
(\mathfrak{t}_{0},M)\in\Delta
has an
R^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-successor
(\mathfrak{t}_{0}^{\prime},M^{\prime},j)\in\Gamma
, then each
(\mathfrak{t}_{1},M)\in\Delta
has an
R^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-successor of the form
(\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{\prime},M^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\Gamma
.

The intuition behind conditions
(
\Diamondblack
-harmony)
and
(witness)
should be clear, and
(bisim)
is needed to ensure that if, for any fixed
M^{\prime}
, all
(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},M^{\prime},j)\in\Gamma
are mutually
\mathsf{ML\!^{n,u}}(\varrho)
-bisimilar, then all
(\mathfrak{t},M)\in\Delta
are also
\mathsf{ML\!^{n,u}}(\varrho)
-bisimilar to each other.
It should also be clear that if
\mathfrak{A}_{1},a_{1}\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{n,u}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{A}_{2},a_{2}
, then every mosaic
M(\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2},a)
, for
a\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{i})
,
i=1,2
, has an
R
-witness for any relevant
R
, namely, the set
\mathcal{M}
of all mosaics
M(\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2},b)
such that
a^{\prime}R^{\mathfrak{A}_{j}}b
, for some
j\in\{1,2\}
and
a^{\prime}\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{j})
with
M(\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2},a)=M(\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2},a^{\prime})
.
Observe also that every
R
-witness
\mathcal{M}
for
M
contains a subset
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
such that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is an
R
-witness for
M
and
|\mathcal{M}^{\prime}|\leq k^{\bullet}2^{|\textit{sub}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})|}
.
So, from now on, we assume all witnesses to be of size
\leq k^{\bullet}2^{|\textit{sub}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})|}
.

We can now define the mosaic elimination procedure for a set
\mathfrak{S}
of mosaics. Call
M=(M_{1},M_{2})\in\mathfrak{S}

bad
in
\mathfrak{S}
if at least one of the following conditions is
not
satisfied for
i=1,2
:

–

if
\Diamond_{R}^{\geq k}\chi\in\mathfrak{t}\in M_{i}
, then there is an
R
-witness for
M
in
\mathfrak{S}
;

–

if
\Diamondblack\chi\in\mathfrak{t}\in M_{i}
, then there are
(M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{S}
and
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in M^{\prime}_{i}
such that
\chi\in\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in M_{i}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
are
\Diamondblack
-equivalent.

Given a set
\mathfrak{S}
of mosaics and
M\in\mathfrak{S}
, it can be decided in double exponential time in
|\varphi_{1}|+|\varphi_{2}|
whether
M
is bad in
\mathfrak{S}
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}_{0}
be the set of all mosaics. Let
h
be a function that, for any
c\in\sigma
and
i\in\{1,2\}
, picks one mosaic
h(N_{c},i)=(M_{1},M_{2})
such that
N_{c}\in\mathfrak{t}
, for some
\mathfrak{t}\in M_{i}
, and if
N_{c^{\prime}}\in\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
, for some
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in M_{j}
,
j\in\{1,2\}
and
c^{\prime}\in\sigma
, then
h(N_{c},i)=h(N_{c^{\prime}},j)
.
By the definition of mosaics,
h(N_{c},1)=h(N_{c},2)
for all
c\in\varrho
.

Given such
h
, let
\mathfrak{S}_{0}^{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{0}
comprise all mosaics of the form
h(N_{c},i)
and those that do not have occurrences of nominals. Starting from
\mathfrak{S}_{0}^{h}
, we construct inductively sets
\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}^{h}
,
n\geq 0
, by looking through the mosaics in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{h}
and eliminating the bad ones. We stop when
\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}^{h}=\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{h}
and set
\mathfrak{S}_{\ast}^{h}=\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{h}
. This can be done in double-exponential time (see the appendix).

Lemma 9
.

The following conditions are equivalent
:

(i)

there exist pointed
\sigma
-structures
\mathfrak{A}_{i},a_{i}
,
i=1,2
, such that
\mathfrak{A}_{i}\models\varphi_{i}(a_{i})
and
\mathfrak{A}_{1},a_{1}\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{n,u}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{A}_{2},a_{2}

(
and so
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
are not
L^{\prime}
-separable
);

(ii)

there exist
h
,
M^{*}=(M^{*}_{1},M^{*}_{2})\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ast}^{h}
and
\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{i}\in M^{*}_{i}
,
i=1,2
, such that
\varphi_{i}\in\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{i}\in M^{*}_{i}
,
h(N_{c},j)=(M_{1},M_{2})\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ast}^{h}
, for all
c\in\sigma
and
j=1,2
, and all types in
M^{*}_{j}\cup M_{j}
are
\Diamondblack
-equivalent to each other.

Here, we only sketch how, given
(ii)
, to construct the required
\mathfrak{A}_{i},a_{i}
. For any
M=(M_{1},M_{2})\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ast}^{h}
and
\Diamond_{R}^{\geq k}\chi\in\mathfrak{t}\in M_{i}
, for some
\Diamond_{R}^{\geq k}\chi
,
\mathfrak{t}
and
i\in\{1,2\}
, we take an
R
-witness
\mathcal{M}_{R,M}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{\ast}^{h}
for
M
with the relation
R^{M,\mathcal{M}_{R,M}}
and define the
{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{i})
as the sets of all words

s=(\mathfrak{t}_{0},M_{0},i_{0})R_{1}(\mathfrak{t}_{1},M_{1},i_{1})R_{2}\dots R_{n}(\mathfrak{t}_{n},M_{n},i_{n})

(1)

such that
\omega>n\geq 0
,
M_{j}=(M_{j,1},M_{j,2})\in\mathfrak{S}_{\ast}^{h}
, for
j\leq n
,

–

\mathfrak{t}_{j}\in M_{j,i}
and
1\leq i_{j}\leq f(M_{j,i})
, for all
j\leq n
;

–

M_{j+1}\in\mathcal{M}_{R_{j+1},M_{j}}
and no nominal occurs in
M_{j+1}
;

–

all types in
M_{k}^{\ast}
and
M_{0,k}
are
\Diamondblack
-equivalent,
k=1,2
.

For a propositional variable
A
and
s
in (
1
), we set
s\in A^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}
iff
A\in\mathfrak{t}_{n}
; for
N_{c}
, we set
c^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}=s
iff
n=0
,
N_{c}\in\mathfrak{t}_{0}
and
h(N_{c},i)=M_{0}
, and for a binary
R\in\sigma
, we set:

–

(s^{\prime},s)\in R^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}
if
s^{\prime}
is obtained by dropping
R_{n}(\mathfrak{t}_{n},M_{n},i_{n})
from
s
,
R=R_{n}
and

(\mathfrak{t}_{n-1},M_{n-1})R_{n}^{M_{n-1},\mathcal{M}_{R_{n},M_{n-1}}}(\mathfrak{t}_{n},M_{n},i_{n});

–

(s,(\mathfrak{t},M,j))\in R^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}
if
(\mathfrak{t},M,j)\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{i})
, a nominal occurs in
M
and
(\mathfrak{t}_{n},M_{n})R^{M_{n},\mathcal{M}_{R,M_{n}}}(\mathfrak{t},M,j)
.

Finally, the bisimulation
\boldsymbol{\beta}
between the
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
is defined by taking
s\boldsymbol{\beta}s^{\prime}
, for
s
in (
1
) and

s^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{t}_{0}^{\prime},M_{0}^{\prime},i_{0}^{\prime})R_{1}^{\prime}\dots R_{m}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{t}_{m}^{\prime},M_{m}^{\prime},i_{m}^{\prime})
,
if
n=m
,
R_{i}=R_{i}^{\prime}
and
M_{i}=M_{i}^{\prime}
, for all
i\leq n
.

Lemma
9
gives a
2\textsc{ExpTime}
upper bound for deciding (Craig)
\mathsf{GML\!^{n,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{n,u}}
-separation, and the construction can be modified to show the same bound for the other logics with
\Diamondblack
.
To establish the
coNExpTime
upper bounds of Theorem
8
for the
\Diamondblack
-free languages, we restrict the
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
to
s
in (
1
) with
n
not exceeding the modal depth of
\varphi_{i}
and such that either
\mathfrak{t}_{0}\in\{\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{1},\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{2}\}
and
M_{0}=M^{*}
or
h(N_{c},i)=M_{0}
, for some
c\in\sigma
.
The size of the resulting structures is exponential in
|\varphi_{1}|+|\varphi_{2}|
and their existence can clearly be checked in
NExpTime
.

By Lemma
5
, it suffices to prove the matching lower bounds for unary Craig separation.
The underlying idea, inspired by
[
29
]
, is to construct polysize formulas
\varphi_{n}
and
\psi_{n}
,
n<\omega
, in the required language such that if
\mathfrak{A}\models\varphi_{n}(a)
,
\mathfrak{B}\models\psi_{n}(b)
, and
\mathfrak{A},a\sim_{L^{\prime}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},b
, then there is
E\subseteq{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{B})

of size
2^{n}
whose elements are
L^{\prime}(\varrho)
-bisimilar and satisfy all pairwise distinct combinations of variables
A_{0},\dots,A_{n-1}\notin\varrho
.
For example,
\varphi_{n}=\Diamond_{R}^{=1}\dots\Diamond_{R}^{=1}\top
with
n
-many
\Diamond_{R}^{=1}
is such that
\mathfrak{A}\models\varphi_{n}(a)
iff
\mathfrak{A}
has a unique
R
-chain of length
n
starting from
a
and

\psi_{n}=\bigwedge_{0\leq i<n}\Big[\Box_{R}^{i}\Diamond_{R}^{=2}\top\land\Box_{R}^{i}(\Diamond_{R}A_{i}\wedge\Diamond_{R}\neg A_{i})\land{}\\[-3.0pt]
\bigwedge_{0\leq j<i}\Box_{R}^{i}\big((A_{j}\rightarrow\Box_{R}A_{j})\wedge(\neg A_{j}\rightarrow\Box_{R}\neg A_{j})\big)\Big].

is such that
\mathfrak{B}\models\psi_{n}(b)
iff the
n
-long
R
-chains from
b
comprise a binary tree of depth
n
whose leaves satisfy binary numbers encoded by the
A_{i}
. So, if
\mathfrak{A}\models\varphi_{n}(a)
,
\mathfrak{B}\models\psi_{n}(b)
, and
\mathfrak{A},a\sim_{\mathsf{ML}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},b
, then there is a unique
a^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{A}
reachable via an
R
-chain of length
n
from
a
, and there are leaves
E=\{e_{0},\dots,e_{2^{n}-1}\}
of the binary tree with root
b
in
\mathfrak{B}
such that
e_{i}
satisfies the binary encoding of
i
via
A_{0},\dots,A_{n-1}
, and so

\mathfrak{A},{a^{\prime}}\sim_{L^{\prime}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},e_{0}\sim_{L^{\prime}(\varrho)}\cdots\sim_{L^{\prime}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},e_{2^{n}-1}
.
The set
E
can now be used, following
[
29
, Secs. 7, 9]
, to encode exponential space-bounded alternating Turing machines for the
2ExpTime
-lower bound, and
2^{n}\times 2^{n}
-torus tiling for the
coNExpTime
one.

VI
Separation in Fragments of
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,u}}

Our next aim is to generalise the mosaic technique of Sec.
V
to nominal-free graded modal logics with inverse and universal modalities and prove the following:

Theorem 10
.

Separation and Craig-separation are

2ExpTime
-complete

for all pairs of the form
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,u}}/L^{\prime}
, where
L^{\prime}\in\{\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}},\mathsf{ML\!^{i}},\mathsf{ML\!^{u}},\mathsf{ML}\}
, and

coNExpTime
-complete

for
\mathsf{GML\!^{i}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{i}}
and
\mathsf{GML\!^{i}}/\mathsf{ML}
.

To show the upper bound for Craig
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}
-separation, suppose
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
are
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,u}}
-formulas,
\sigma=\mathit{sig}(\varphi_{1})\cup\mathit{sig}(\varphi_{2})
and
\varrho=\mathit{sig}(\varphi_{1})\cap\mathit{sig}(\varphi_{2})
. Our task is to check the existence of pointed
\sigma
-structures
\mathfrak{A}_{i},a_{i}
,
i=1,2
, and an
\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}(\varrho)
-bisimulation
\boldsymbol{\beta}
between
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{A}_{2}
such that
\mathfrak{A}_{1}\models\varphi_{1}(a_{1})
,
\mathfrak{A}_{2}\models\varphi_{2}(a_{2})
and
a_{1}\boldsymbol{\beta}a_{2}
.
We use the notion of type from Sec.
V
adapted in the obvious way for
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,u}}
.

Example 11
.

Consider the
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,u}}
-formulas

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}=B\land\blacksquare\big(B\rightarrow(\neg A\wedge\Diamond^{=1}_{R}\top\wedge\neg\Diamond_{R^{-}}\top\wedge\Diamond_{R}A)\big)\land{}

\displaystyle\hskip 51.78421pt\blacksquare\big(A\rightarrow(\Diamond_{R}^{=1}\top\wedge\Diamond_{R^{-}}^{=1}\top\wedge\Diamond_{R}\neg A)\big)\land{}

\displaystyle\hskip 79.6678pt\blacksquare\big((\neg A\wedge\neg B)\rightarrow(\Diamond_{R}^{=1}\top\wedge\Diamond_{R^{-}}^{=1}\top\wedge\Diamond_{R}A)\big),

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}=\Diamond^{=2}_{R}A\land\blacksquare\big(A\rightarrow\Diamond_{R}^{=2}\neg A\wedge(\neg\Diamond_{R^{-1}}B\rightarrow\Diamond_{R^{-}}^{=2}\neg A)\big)\land{}

\displaystyle\hskip 110.96556pt\blacksquare\big((\neg A\wedge\neg B)\rightarrow(\Diamond_{R}^{=2}A\wedge\Diamond_{R^{-}}^{=2}A)\big).

It is not hard to see that
\varphi_{1}\models\neg\varphi_{2}
. The picture below shows structures
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{A}_{2}
(with all arrows labelled by
R
) that satisfy
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
at their respective roots. The relation
\boldsymbol{\beta}
defined by taking
a\boldsymbol{\beta}b
iff
a
and

are in the same dashed ellipse is an
\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}
-bisimulation between the
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
, and so, by Lemma
4
,
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
are not
\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}
-separable. Note that all
\mathfrak{t}(\mathfrak{A}_{1},a_{2n})
with
n>0
coincide as well as all
\mathfrak{t}(\mathfrak{A}_{1},a_{2n+1})
with
n>1
. However, we have
B\in\mathfrak{t}(\mathfrak{A}_{1},a_{0})\neq\mathfrak{t}(\mathfrak{A}_{1},a_{2})\notni B
and
\Diamond_{R^{-}}B\in\mathfrak{t}(\mathfrak{A}_{1},a_{1})\neq\mathfrak{t}(\mathfrak{A}_{1},a_{3})\notni\Diamond_{R^{-}}B
. Also, all
\mathfrak{t}(\mathfrak{A}_{2},e_{2nl})
with
n>0
,
l=1,2
coincide as well as all
\mathfrak{t}(\mathfrak{A}_{2},e_{2n+1l})
, for
n>1
,
l=1,2
.
\dashv

\mathfrak{A}_{1}
\varphi_{1}

B
a_{0}
A
a_{1}
a_{2}
A
a_{3}
\dots
\mathfrak{A}_{2}
\varphi_{2}

B
e_{0}
A
e_{11}
A
e_{12}
e_{21}

e_{31}
A
e_{32}
\dots
\dots

As in Sec.
V
,
\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}
(if they exist) are assembled from mosaics, which have to be appropriately modified to deal with inverse modalities. To this end, we supply each node with some information about the types of its ‘parents’ and ‘children’​​. Following
[
12
, Sec. 8.3]
, we call the result a star-type.

To give a precise definition, we require a few auxiliary notions.
A
successor type set

\mathfrak{s}
contains, for each type
\mathfrak{t}
and each
S\in\{R,R^{-}\}
with
R\in\sigma
, exactly one expression of the form
\Diamond_{S}^{=n}\mathfrak{t}
, for
0\leq n\leq k^{\bullet}
, or
\Diamond_{S}^{>k^{\bullet}}\mathfrak{t}
. Let
a\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})
,
D\subseteq{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})
and
n(a,S,\mathfrak{t},D)=|\{b\in D\mid(a,b)\in S^{\mathfrak{A}},\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}(\mathfrak{A},b)\}|
. The
successor type set
\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{A},a,D)
defined by
a
and
D
contains

–

\Diamond_{S}^{=n}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{A},a,D)
if
n=n(a,S,\mathfrak{t},D)\leq k^{\bullet}
, and

–

\Diamond_{S}^{>k^{\bullet}}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{A},a,D)
if
n(a,S,\mathfrak{t},D)>k^{\bullet}
.

If we are only interested in the existence of an
S
-successor with a type
\mathfrak{t}
, we use
\Diamond_{S}^{>0}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}
as a shorthand for ‘
\Diamond_{S}^{=n}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}

for
n>0
or
\Diamond_{S}^{>k^{\bullet}}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}
’​. We do not mention elements
\Diamond^{=0}_{S}\mathfrak{t}
in
\mathfrak{s}
and omit
\mathfrak{A}
from
\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{A},a,D)
,
\mathfrak{t}(\mathfrak{A},a)
, etc., if understood.

A
star-type
takes the form
\mathfrak{st}=(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})
, where
\mathfrak{t}
is a type,
\mathfrak{s}_{p}
a
parent
successor type set, and
\mathfrak{s}_{c}
a
child
successor type set. To be coherent with the semantics of graded modalities,
\mathfrak{st}
should satisfy some natural conditions defined below.
The
S
-profile
P_{S}(\mathfrak{s})
of
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c}\}
contains, for any
\chi\in\textit{sub}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
,

–

\Diamond_{S}^{=n}\chi
if
n
is the sum of all
k
with
\Diamond_{S}^{=k}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}
and
\chi\in\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
;

–

\Diamond_{S}^{>k^{\bullet}}\chi
if this sum is
>k^{\bullet}
or
\Diamond_{S}^{>k^{\bullet}}t^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}
, for some
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\ni\chi
.

The
S
-profile
P_{S}(\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})
of
\mathfrak{s}_{p}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{c}
contains, for all formulas
\chi\in\textit{sub}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
,

–

\Diamond_{S}^{=n}\chi
if
n=n_{1}+n_{2}\leq k^{\bullet}
for
\Diamond_{S}^{=n_{1}}\chi
in the
S
-profile of
\mathfrak{s}_{p}
and
\Diamond_{S}^{=n_{2}}\chi
in the
S
-profile of
\mathfrak{s}_{c}
;

–

\Diamond_{S}^{>k^{\bullet}}\chi
otherwise.

A star-type
\mathfrak{st}=(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})
is called
coherent
if, for any formula
\Diamond_{S}^{\geq n}\chi\in\textit{sub}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
, we have
\Diamond_{S}^{\geq n}\chi\in\mathfrak{t}
iff
P_{S}(\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})
contains either
\Diamond_{S}^{=n^{\prime}}\chi
, for
some
n^{\prime}\geq n
, or
\Diamond_{S}^{>k^{\bullet}}\chi
. Henceforth, we assume all star-types to be coherent.

The
star-type realised by
a\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})
and
D\subseteq{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})
in
\mathfrak{A}
is the triple

\mathfrak{st}(a,D)=\big(\mathfrak{t}(a),\mathfrak{s}(a,D),\mathfrak{s}(a,N(a)\setminus D)\big)
, where
N(a)=\{b\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})\mid(a,b)\in S^{\mathfrak{A}},\ S\in\{R,R^{-}\},\ R\in\sigma\}
is the
neighbourhood of
a
in
\mathfrak{A}
.

Example 12
.

The picture below illustrates 4 star-types realised
by
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{A}_{2}

\text{ for some }a^{\prime}\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}(\varrho)}a\}.

Alternative and possibly larger witnesses can be obtained by dropping the restrictions ‘

Example 14
.

,

\dashv

Compared to the mosaic construction in Sec.
V
, it is much harder now to show that
({\dagger})
it suffices to use mosaics and witnesses of exponential size and that
({\ddagger})
checking whether a given exponential set of such mosaics is a witness for a given mosaic can be done in double exponential time. Here, by the
size
of a mosaic
M=(M_{1},M_{2})
we mean
|M|=|M_{1}|+|M_{2}|
.

Call successor sets
\mathfrak{s}
and
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}

profile-equivalent
if they have the same
S
-profiles for all
S\in\{R,R^{-}\}
with
R\in\sigma
. Star-types
\mathfrak{st}=(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})
and
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime})
are
parent profile-equivalent
if
\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{p}
,
\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime}
are profile equivalent;
\mathfrak{st}
and
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}
are
child profile-equivalent
if
\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{c}
,
\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime}
are profile equivalent.
Mosaics
M=(M_{1},M_{2})
and
M^{\prime}=(M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})
are
child profile-equivalent
if, for any
\mathfrak{st}\in M_{i}
, there exists a child-profile equivalent
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}\in M_{i}^{\prime}
, and vice versa, for
i=1,2
;
M
and
M^{\prime}
are
parent profile-equivalent
if, for any
\mathfrak{st}\in M_{i}
, there exists a parent-profile equivalent
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}\in M_{i}^{\prime}
, and vice versa, for
i=1,2
.
Observe that the number of child/parent profile non-equivalent star-types does not exceed
(|\varphi_{1}|+|\varphi_{2}|)^{4(|\varphi_{1}|+|\varphi_{2}|)}
. The following lemma, proved in the appendix, establishes property
({\dagger})
above using a syntactic characterisation of witnesses, which is efficient in the sense that it also guarantees
({\ddagger})
:

Lemma 15
.

There is a polytime computable number
k_{\varphi_{1}\varphi_{2}}=O(2^{|\varphi_{1}|+|\varphi_{2}|})
such that, for any mosaic
M=(M_{1},M_{2})
and witness
\mathcal{M}
for
M
, there exist a mosaic
N=(N_{1},N_{2})
and a witness
\mathcal{N}
for
N
satisfying the following
:

–

|N|
,
|\mathcal{N}|
, and all
|N^{\prime}|
with
N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
do not exceed
k_{\varphi_{1}\varphi_{2}}
,

–

M
and
N
are parent profile-equivalent,

–

every
N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
has a child profile-equivalent
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
,

–

N_{i}\subseteq M_{i}
and, for every
N^{\prime}=(N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}
, there is
M^{\prime}=(M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{M}
with
N_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq M_{i}^{\prime}
, for
i=1,2
.

Thus, in our mosaic elimination procedure establishing the
2\textsc{ExpTime}
upper bound in Theorem
10
, instead of any mosaic
M
and its witness
\mathcal{M}
we can use exponential-size
N
and
\mathcal{N}
provided by Lemma
15
.
Let
\mathfrak{S}_{0}
be the set of all mosaics
M=(M_{1},M_{2})
with non-empty
M_{1}
,
M_{2}
and
|M|\leq k_{\varphi_{1}\varphi_{2}}^{2}
. Clearly
\mathfrak{S}_{0}
can be computed in double exponential time in
|\varphi_{1}|+|\varphi_{2}|
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{0}
. We call
M\in\mathfrak{S}

bad
in
\mathfrak{S}
if at least one of the following conditions does not hold:

–

there is a witness
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}
for
M
with
|\mathcal{M}|\leq k_{\varphi_{1}\varphi_{2}}
;

–

if
\Diamondblack\chi\in\mathfrak{t}
and
(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})\in M_{i}
, for
(M_{1},M_{2})\in\mathfrak{S}
,

, then there is a root mosaic
(M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{S}
such that
\chi\in\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
, for some
(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime})\in M_{i}^{\prime}
with
\Diamondblack
-equivalent
\mathfrak{t}
,
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
.

Using (
{\dagger})
and
({\ddagger})
, we now compute in double exponential time a sequence
\mathfrak{S}_{0},\mathfrak{S}_{1},\dots
, where each
\mathfrak{S}_{i+1}
eliminates all bad mosaics from
\mathfrak{S}_{i}
, stop when
\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}=\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and set
\mathfrak{S}^{\ast}=\mathfrak{S}_{n}
.

Lemma 16
.

The following conditions are equivalent
:

(i)

there exist pointed
\sigma
-structures
\mathfrak{A}_{i},a_{i}
, for
i=1,2
, such that
\mathfrak{A}_{i}\models\varphi_{i}(a_{i})
and
\mathfrak{A}_{1},a_{1}\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{A}_{2},a_{2}
;

(ii)

there exist a root mosaic
M^{*}=(M^{*}_{1},M^{*}_{2})\in\mathfrak{S}^{\ast}
with
\varphi_{1}\in\mathfrak{t}
and
\varphi_{2}\in\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
, for some
(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})\in M^{*}_{1}
and
(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime})\in M^{*}_{2}
.

Proof.

The proof of
(i)\Rightarrow(ii)
needs more work compared to the proof of Lemma
9
. Given
\mathfrak{A}_{i},a_{i}
,
i=1,2
, satisfying
(i)
, we consider the set of all pairs
p=(M,\mathcal{M}_{M})
such that
\mathcal{M}_{M}
is a witness for
M
given by the
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
. The union of all such
\{M\}\cup\mathcal{M}_{M}
would not contain bad mosaics if
M
and
\mathcal{M}_{M}
were of size
\leq k_{\varphi_{1}\varphi_{2}}
, which is not necessarily the case. For
p=(M,\mathcal{M}_{M})
of the wrong size, we use Lemma
15
to obtain
(M^{p},\mathcal{M}_{M}^{p})
satisfying the conditions of that lemma. However, these pairs are not necessarily defined by the
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
. We show in the appendix that one can construct from the
(M^{p},\mathcal{M}_{M}^{p})
a set
\mathfrak{S}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{0}
that does not contain any bad mosaics.

(ii)\Rightarrow(i)

As in the proof of Lemma
9
, we build the required
\mathfrak{A}_{i},a_{i}
,
i=1,2
, and
\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}(\varrho)
-bisimulation
\boldsymbol{\beta}
between them using
\mathfrak{S}^{*}
provided by
(ii)
, though the construction is somewhat different. For every
M\in\mathfrak{S}^{\ast}
, take a witness
\mathcal{M}_{M}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}^{\ast}
for
M
together with the corresponding relations
S^{M,\mathcal{M}_{M}}\subseteq\Delta^{M}\times\Gamma^{M}
between
\Delta^{M}=\Delta_{1}^{M}\cup\Delta_{2}^{M}
and
\Gamma^{M}=\Gamma_{1}^{M}\cup\Gamma_{2}^{M}
.
For
i=1,2
, the domain
{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{i})
of
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
comprises the words of the form

s=M_{0}\dots M_{n-1}(\mathfrak{st}_{n},j_{n},M_{n}),

(2)

where
M_{j}=(M_{j,1},M_{j,2})\in\mathfrak{S}^{\ast}
,
0\leq j\leq n
,
M_{0}
is a root mosaic, with any type in
M_{0,i}
being
\Diamondblack
-equivalent to any type in
M^{\ast}_{i}
,
M_{j+1}\in\mathcal{M}_{M_{j}}
, for all
j<n
,
\mathfrak{st}_{n}\in M_{n,i}
and
j_{n}<\omega
.
It follows from the definition of (bad) mosaics that all types used in the construction of
{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{i})
are
\Diamondblack
-equivalent to each other.

Let
\mathfrak{t}_{n}
be the type of
\mathfrak{st}_{n}
, in which case
\mathfrak{t}_{n}
is also called the
type
of
s
. We interpret the propositional variables
A\in\sigma
by taking
s\in A^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}
if
A\in\mathfrak{t}_{n}
. For any
R\in\sigma
, we set

–

(s^{\prime},s)\in R^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}
for any
s
in (
2
) and any
s^{\prime}
of the form

s^{\prime}=M_{0}\dots M_{n-2}(\mathfrak{st}_{n-1},j_{n-1},M_{n-1})

(3)

such that
(\mathfrak{st}_{n-1},j_{n-1})R^{M_{n-1},\mathcal{M}_{M_{n-1}}}(\mathfrak{st}_{n},j_{n},M_{n})
;

–

(s,s^{\prime})\in R^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}
for any
s
of the form (
2
) and any
s^{\prime}
of the form (
3
)
such that
(\mathfrak{st}_{n-1},j_{n-1}){(R^{-})}^{M_{n-1},\mathcal{M}_{M_{n-1}}}(\mathfrak{st}_{n},j_{n},M_{n})
.

One can show by induction (see the appendix for details) that, for any
\chi\in\textit{sub}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
and any
s
in (
2
), we have
\mathfrak{A}_{i}\models\chi(s)
iff
\chi\in\mathfrak{t}_{n}
.

We next define
\boldsymbol{\beta}\subseteq{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{1})\times{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{2})
by taking
s\boldsymbol{\beta}s^{\prime}
, for
s\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{1})
of the from (
2
) and

of the form

s^{\prime}=M_{0}^{\prime}\dots M_{m-1}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{st}_{m}^{\prime},j_{m}^{\prime},M_{m}^{\prime})

if
n=m
and
M_{j}=M_{j}^{\prime}
for all

. It follows from non-emptiness of mosaics in
\mathfrak{S}^{*}
, the definition of
R^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}
and conditions

)
and

2
)
that

is a
