\vdots

In this example, we have
I_{0}=-(0,q_{1},q_{1})
,
I_{1}=+(0,q_{4})
and
I_{4}=-(0,q_{2},q_{4})
. So the machine starts in state
q_{0}
by decrementing the (already empty) register
0
, and moves to state
q_{1}
. In this state, it increments register 0, and continues to state
q_{4}
. In
q_{4}
, it first decrements register 0 while staying in
q_{4}
. Then it tries to decrement register 0 again, but now that register is empty so the next state is
q_{2}
instead of
q_{4}
.

It is straightforward (if somewhat labour intensive) to verify that the intended pointed models satisfy
\varphi_{M}
and
\psi_{M}
, and that they are
\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}(\varrho)
-bisimilar.

Proposition 32
.

If a machine
M
is non-halting, then there are pointed models
\mathfrak{A},x_{0}
of
\varphi_{M}
and
\mathfrak{B},y_{0}
of
\psi_{M}
such that
\mathfrak{A},x_{0}\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},y_{0}
.

Next, we need to show the converse:

Proposition 33
.

If there are pointed models
\mathfrak{A},x_{0}
of
\varphi_{M}
and
\mathfrak{B},y_{0}
of
\psi_{M}
such that
\mathfrak{A},x_{0}\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},y_{0}
,
then
M
is non-halting.

Proof.

We show that
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
contain intended models as sub-models. Because it is a model of
\varphi_{M}
,
\mathfrak{A}
contains a sequence of elements
x_{0},x_{1},\dots
, where
x_{0}=c
,
x_{t+1}
is the unique
R
-successor of
x_{t}
, and
x_{t}
is the unique
R
-predecessor of
x_{t+1}
. Furthermore, on each
x_{t}
exactly one
q_{i}
(with
0\leq i\leq\ell
) holds.

In model
\mathfrak{B}
, we also have
y_{0}=c
. Let
Y_{0}=\{y_{0}\}
, and for every
t
, let
Y_{t+1}
be the
R
-successors of the elements of
Y_{t}
. Let
\boldsymbol{\beta}\subseteq{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})\times{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{B})
be the
\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}(\varrho)
-bisimulation between
\mathfrak{A},x_{0}
and
\mathfrak{B},y_{0}
. The proof now proceeds through a series of claims.

Claim 1.
For every
t\in\omega
,
(i)
if
(x_{t},y)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
, then
y\in Y_{t}
, and
(ii)
if
y\in Y_{t}
, then
(x_{t},y)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
.

Proof of Claim 1.
We proceed by induction on
t
. As base case, note that
x_{0}
and
y_{0}
are the constants
c
in their respective models, so
x_{0}
and
y_{0}
can only be
\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}(\varrho)
-bisimilar to each other. Since
\boldsymbol{\beta}
is, by assumption, an
\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}(\varrho)
-bisimulation, we must have
(x_{0},y_{0})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
. Assume then as the induction hypothesis that
t>0
and that the claim holds for all
t^{\prime}<t
.

Take any
y
such that
(x_{t},y)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
. Because
\boldsymbol{\beta}
is an
\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}(\varrho)
-bisimulation and
x_{t}
has
x_{t-1}
as an
R
-predecessor,
y
must have some
y^{\prime}
as an
R
-predecessor such that
(t_{x-1},y^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
. By the induction hypothesis,
y^{\prime}\in Y_{t-1}
. This implies that
y
is an
R
-successor of an element from
Y_{t-1}
, and so, by definition, we have
y\in Y_{t}
.

Now, take any
y\in Y_{t}
. By the definition of
Y_{t}
, there is some
y^{\prime}\in Y_{t-1}
that is an
r
-predecessor of
y
. By the induction hypothesis,
(x_{t-1},y)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
. Because
\boldsymbol{\beta}
is a bisimulation and
y
is an
R
-successor of
y^{\prime}
, there must be some
x^{\prime}
that is an
R
-successor of
x_{t-1}
such that
(x^{\prime},y)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
. The only
R
-successor of
x_{t-1}
is
x_{t}
, so
x^{\prime}=x_{t}
, and hence
(x_{t},y)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
. This completes the induction step, and thereby the proof of Claim 1.

Claim 2.
For every
y\in Y_{t}
, if
y^{\prime}
is an
R
-predecessor of
y
, then
y^{\prime}\in Y_{t-1}
.

Proof of Claim 2.
By Claim 1,
(x_{t},y)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
. Since
y^{\prime}
is an
R
-predecessor of
y
, we must have
(y^{\prime},x^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
for some
R
-predecessor
x^{\prime}
of
x_{t}
. The only
R
-predecessor of
x_{t}
is
x_{t-1}
, so we have
(x_{t-1},y^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
. By Claim 1, this implies that
y^{\prime}\in Y_{t-1}
.

Claim 3.
For every
t
, there is exactly one
i_{t}
such that
q_{i_{t}}
holds on any of
x_{t}
and
Y_{t}
. This
q_{i_{t}}
holds on all of
x_{t}
and
Y_{t}
. Furthermore,
e_{\ell}
either holds on all of
x_{t}
and
Y_{t}
, or on none.

Proof of Claim 3.
Because
\mathfrak{A}
is a model of
\varphi_{M}
, every
x_{t}
satisfies exactly one
q_{i_{t}}
. The rest of the claim follows immediately from
\boldsymbol{\beta}
being a bisimulation.

Before continuing with further claims, we define
Y_{t}^{\ell}
, for
t>0
and
\ell\in\{0,1\}
, by taking
Y_{t}^{\ell}=\{y\in Y_{t}\mid\mathfrak{B},y\models r_{\ell}\}
. If
\ell\in\{0,1\}
, we write
\overline{\ell}
for
1-\ell
, i.e.,
\overline{0}=1
and
\overline{1}=0
.

Claim 4.
For
\ell\in\{0,1\}
and
t>0
, the
R
-successors of
Y_{t}^{\ell}
are in
Y_{t+1}^{\ell}
and the
R
-predecessors of
Y_{t+1}^{\ell}
are in
Y_{t}^{\ell}
.

Proof of Claim 4.
Conjunct (
7
) says that
r_{\ell}
propagates forward and backward trough
R
.

Claim 5.
For every
t>0
,

–

if
I_{i_{t}}=+(\ell,q_{j})
, then
i_{t+1}=j
and
|Y_{t+1}^{p}\ell=2\times|Y_{t}^{\ell}|
,

–

if
I_{i_{t}}=-(\ell,q_{j},q_{k})
and
e_{\ell}
holds on
x_{t}
, then
i_{t+1}=j
and
|Y_{t+1}^{\ell}|=|Y_{t}^{\ell}|
,

–

if
I_{i_{t}}=-(\ell,q_{j},q_{k})
and
e_{\ell}
does not hold on
x_{t}
, then
i_{t+1}=k
and
|Y_{t+1}^{\ell}|=\frac{1}{2}\times|Y_{t}^{\ell}|
,

and, in each case,
|Y_{t+1}^{\overline{\ell}}|=|Y_{t}^{\overline{\ell}}|
.

Proof of Claim 5.
Conjuncts (
7
), (
8
) and (
9
) of
\varphi_{M}
guarantee that
i_{t+1}
has the appropriate value, i.e., the state transitions as represented in the models
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
follow the rules of
M
.

Consider now the sets
Y_{t}^{\ell}
and
Y_{t+1}^{\ell}
. By Claim 4, all the
R
-successor of
Y_{t}^{\ell}
are in
Y_{t+1}^{\ell}
, and all the
R
-predecessors of
Y_{t+1}^{\ell}
are in
Y_{t}^{\ell}
. Furthermore, conjunct (
9
) of
\psi_{M}
implies that, if
I_{i+t}=+(\ell,q_{j})
, then every
y\in Y_{t}^{\ell}
has exactly two
R
-successors that each have exactly one
R
-predecessor. It follows that
|Y_{t+1}^{\ell}|=2\times|Y_{t}^{\ell}|
. Similarly, if
I_{i_{t}}=-(\ell,q_{j},q_{k})
and
e_{\ell}
holds, then conjunct (
11
) says that every
y\in Y_{t}^{\ell}
has exactly one
R
-successor that has exactly one
R
-predecessor, so
|Y_{t+1}^{\ell}|=|Y_{t}^{\ell}|
. If
I_{i_{t}}=-(\ell,q_{j},q_{k})
and
e_{\ell}
does not hold, then conjunct (
12
) makes sure that every
y\in Y_{t}^{\ell}
has exactly one
R
-successor that has exactly two
R
-predecessors, so
|Y_{t+1}^{\ell}|=\frac{1}{2}\times|Y_{t}^{\ell}|
.

Finally, conjuncts (
10
), (
13
) guarantee that every
y\in Y_{t}^{\overline{\ell}}
has exactly one
R
-successor that has exactly one
R
-predecessor, so
|Y_{t+1}^{\overline{\ell}}|=|Y_{t}^{\overline{\ell}}|
.

Claim 6.

|Y_{1}^{0}|=|Y_{1}^{1}|=1
.

Proof of Claim 6.
By conjuncts (
4
) and (
5
) of
\psi_{M}
,
c
has exactly two successors, one of which satisfies
u\wedge r_{0}
while the other satisfies
u\wedge r_{1}
.

Claim 7.
For any
t>0
and
\ell\in\{0,1\}
, there is exactly one
y\in Y_{t}^{\ell}
that satisfies
u
.

Proof of Claim 7.
For
t=1
, the claim holds because, by conjunct (
5
) of
\psi_{M}
, there is at least one
y\in Y_{1}^{\ell}
that satisfies
u
. As
Y_{1}^{\ell}
is a singleton (by Claim 6), there must be exactly one such
y
. Conjunct (
8
) implies that every
u
-element (other than
c
) has exactly one
R
-predecessor and one
R
-successor that satisfy
u
, so if
Y_{t}^{\ell}
has exactly one element satisfying
u
, then so does
Y_{t+1}^{\ell}
.

Claim 8.
For any
t>0
and
\ell\in\{0,1\}
,

–

if
e_{\ell}
holds on
x_{t}
and
Y_{t}
, then
Y_{t}^{\ell}
is a singleton and

–

if
I_{t}=-(\ell,q_{j},q_{k})
and
Y_{t}^{\ell}
is a singleton, then
e_{\ell}
holds on
x_{t}
and
Y_{t}
.

Proof of Claim 8.
Suppose that
e_{\ell}
holds on
x_{t}
and
Y_{t}
. Then
e_{\ell}\land r_{\ell}
is true on every element of
Y_{t}^{\ell}
. By conjunct (
14
), this implies that
u
is true on all of
Y_{t}^{\ell}
. Claim 7 says that only one element of
Y_{t}^{p}
can satisfy
u
, so
Y_{t}^{\ell}
must be a singleton.

Suppose next that
I_{t}=-(\ell,q_{j},q_{k})
and
Y_{t}^{\ell}
is a singleton. Assume (towards a contradiction) that
e_{\ell}
does not hold on
x_{t}
. Then, by Claim 5,
|Y_{t+1}^{\ell}|=\frac{1}{2}\times|Y_{t}^{\ell}|
. But
Y_{t+1}^{\ell}
cannot have half as many elements as
Y_{t}^{\ell}
, since
Y_{t}^{\ell}
is a singleton. From this contradiction, we conclude that
e_{\ell}
holds on
x_{t}
(and therefore also on
Y_{t}
).

Claim 9.
For any
t
and
\ell\in\{0,1\}
, let
v^{\ell}_{t}
be the value in register
\ell
at time
t
in the run of
M
and
q_{i_{t}}
the state of
M
at time
t
. Then

–

q_{i_{t}}
holds on
x_{t}
and
Y_{t}
, and

–

if
t>0
, then
|Y_{t}^{\ell}|=2^{v_{t}^{\ell}}
.

Proof of Claim 9.
As we remarked when we introduced them, conjuncts (
7
)–(
9
) of
\varphi_{M}
guarantee that the state transition instructions of
M
are obeyed, if
e_{\ell}
holds in the appropriate places. Claim 5 shows that
\mathfrak{B}
follows the incrementation/decrementation instructions of
M
, again under the assumption that
e_{\ell}
holds where appropriate. Finally, Claim 8 shows that
e_{\ell}
holds where appropriate.

Claim 10.

M
is non-halting.

Proof of Claim 10.
Follows from conjunct (
10
) of
\phi_{M}
together with Claim 9.

This completes the proof of Proposition
33
.

\dashv

Thus, we have shown that Craig
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}
-separation is RE-complete. This is also true of unary Craig
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}
-separation because
\varphi_{M}
and
\psi_{M}
have the same binary predicates. And using the second claim of Lemma
5
, we obtain that
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}
-separation is RE-complete.

By using the so-called spy-point technique
[
39
]
, we can adapt the proof to the case of (Craig)
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n}}
-separation (without the universal diamond
\Diamondblack
and its dual universal box
\blacksquare
). Indeed, let
\varphi_{M}
and
\psi_{M}
be as defined above, except that we replace
\blacksquare
by
\square_{U}
and add the following conjuncts to both of the resulting formulas:

\Diamond\!_{U}N_{c},\quad\square_{U}(\square_{R}\Diamond_{U^{-1}}N_{c}\wedge\square_{R^{-1}}\Diamond_{U^{-1}}N_{c}).

These additional conjuncts guarantees that, while
U
is not necessarily interpreted as the universal relation, all elements that are relevant in the proof are
U
-successors of
c
, and hence all conjuncts that start with
\square_{U}
have the desired effect.

To deal with the nominal-free
L^{\prime}
, we can simulate
N_{c}
by adding to
\varphi_{M}
the conjunct
N_{c}\leftrightarrow p
with a fresh propositional variable
p
and replace
N_{c}
in
\psi_{M}
with
p
, making it shared in
\varphi_{M}
and
\psi_{M}
.

This proves the second item of Theorem
7
.

-B

\mathsf{C^{2}}/\mathsf{FO^{2}}
-separation

We now show the first item of Theorem
7
. Our reduction is very similar to the one given in the previous subsection for
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}
-separation. We begin by discussing the required modifications.

We again construct two formulas,
\xi_{M}
and
\chi_{M}
, such that the pointed model
\mathfrak{A},x_{0}
of
\xi_{M}
is essentially an
R
-chain
encoding the the run of
M
, and the model
\mathfrak{B},y_{0}
of
\chi_{M}
represents the contents of the registers. Synchronisation between
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
is done through the bisimulation between them. All of this is exactly as in the
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}
case.
Observe, however, that now we deal with a
\mathsf{FO^{2}}(\varrho)
-bisimulation, which is more constraining than an
\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}(\varrho)
-bisimulation. Recall that a
\mathsf{FO^{2}}(\varrho)
-bisimulation requires that if
(x,y)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
, then, for every
x^{\prime}
, there is an
y^{\prime}
such that
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
(x,x^{\prime})\mapsto(y,y^{\prime})
is a partial isomorphism, and similarly, for every
y^{\prime}
, there must be an
x^{\prime}
with the same properties. Here,
(x,x^{\prime})\mapsto(y,y^{\prime})
being a partial isomorphism means that (i) for every unary predicate
P
in the relevant signature,
P(x)
iff
P(y)
and
P(x^{\prime})
iff
P(y^{\prime})
, (ii) for every binary predicate
R
in the relevant signature,
R(x,x^{\prime})
iff
R(y,y^{\prime})
and
R(x^{\prime},x)
iff
R(y^{\prime},y^{\prime})
, and (iii)
x=x^{\prime}
iff
y=y^{\prime}
.

Perhaps surprisingly, it is only condition (iii) that will give us trouble, and that requires us to make the current proof slightly more complicated than the
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{i,n,u}}
one. In the intended pointed models of
\varphi_{M}
and
\psi_{M}
, we can already choose a
y^{\prime}
(or
x^{\prime}
) that satisfies properties (i) and (ii). Condition (iii), however, is not satisfied for those models.
This is because condition (iii) implies that an
\mathsf{FO^{2}}
-bisimulation can never relate a singleton set to a multi-element set (i.e., if
(x,y)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
(x,y^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
with
y\not=y^{\prime}
, then there must be a
x^{\prime}\not=x
such that
(x^{\prime},y)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
). After all, if
(x,y),(x,y^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
with
y\not=y^{\prime}
, then there must be some
x^{\prime}
such that
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
(x,x^{\prime})\mapsto(y,y^{\prime})
is a partial isomorphism. In particular, we must then have
x=x^{\prime}
iff
y=y^{\prime}
, so from
y\not=y^{\prime}
it follows that
x\not=x^{\prime}
.

In order to address this issue, we make a small modification to the intended models for
\varphi_{M}
and
\psi_{M}
. Specifically, we create two copies of everything: the intended models of
\xi_{M}
and
\chi_{M}
are simply two disjoint copies of the models for
\varphi_{M}
and
\psi_{M}
, respectively.
Now, let us consider the precise formulas
\xi_{M}
and
\chi_{M}
that achieve this effect. We begin with
\xi_{M}
, which is the conjunction of the following formulas:

1.

\exists^{=2}x\,q_{0}(x)
,

2.

\forall x\,\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq m}q_{i}(x)
,

3.

\forall x\,\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq m}(q_{i}(x)\rightarrow\bigwedge_{i<j\leq m}\neg q_{j}(x))
,

4.

\forall x\,(q_{0}(x)\rightarrow\forall y\neg R(y,x))
,

5.

\forall x\,\exists^{=1}yR(x,y)
,

6.

\forall x\,\neg q_{0}(x)\rightarrow\exists^{=1}yR(y,x)
;

if
I_{i}=+(\ell,q_{j})
, then

7.

\forall x\,(q_{i}(x)\rightarrow\forall y\,(R(x,y)\rightarrow q_{j}(y)))
,

and if
I_{i}=-(\ell,q_{j},q_{k})
, then

8.

\forall x\,((q_{i}(x)\wedge e_{\ell}(x))\rightarrow\forall y\,(R(x,y)\rightarrow q_{j}(y)))
,

9.

\forall x\,((q_{i}(x)\wedge\neg e_{\ell}(x))\rightarrow\forall y\,(R(x,y)\rightarrow q_{k}(y)))
;

and, finally,

10.

\forall x\,\neg q_{m}(x)

The reader may notice that most conjuncts of
\xi_{M}
are simply translations of the conjunct of
\varphi_{M}
with the same number to
\mathsf{C^{2}}
. The only exceptions, and this is a very minor exception, is conjuncts (
1
). The corresponding
\varphi_{M}
conjuncts guarantee the existence of one
q_{0}
element, while here we guarantee the existence of two
q_{0}
elements.

The conjuncts of
\chi_{M}
are as follows:

1.

\exists^{=2}x\,q_{0}(x)
,

2.

\forall x\,\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq m}q_{i}(x)
,

3.

\forall x\,\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq m}(q_{i}(x)\rightarrow\bigwedge_{i<j\leq m}\neg q_{j}(x))
,

4.

\forall x\,(q_{0}(x)\rightarrow\exists^{=2}y\,R(x,y))
,

5.

\forall x\,(q_{0}(x)\rightarrow\bigwedge_{\ell\in\{0,1\}}\exists^{=1}y\,(R(x,y)\wedge u(y)\wedge r_{\ell}(y)))
,

6.

\forall x\,(q_{0}(x)\rightarrow(e_{0}(x)\wedge e_{1}(x)))
,

7.

\forall x\,(r_{\ell}(x)\rightarrow(\forall y\,((R(x,y)\vee R(y,x))\rightarrow(r_{\ell}(y)\vee q_{0}(y)))))
,

8.

\forall x\,((u(x)\wedge\neg q_{0}(x))\rightarrow(\exists^{=1}y\,(R(x,y)\wedge u(y))\wedge{}\\
\hskip 133.72786pt\exists^{=1}y\,(R(y,x)\wedge u(y))))
;

if
I_{i}=+(\ell,q_{j})
, then

9.

\forall x\,((q_{i}(x)\wedge r_{\ell}(x))\rightarrow(\exists^{=2}y\,R(x,y)\wedge\forall y\,(R(x,y)\rightarrow{}\\
\hskip 167.87108pt\exists^{=1}x\,R(x,y))))
,

10.

\forall x\,((q_{i}(x)\wedge\neg r_{\ell}(x))\rightarrow(\exists^{=1}y\,R(x,y)\wedge\forall y\,(R(x,y)\rightarrow{}\\
\hskip 167.87108pt\exists^{=1}x\,R(x,y))))
;

if
I_{i}=-(\ell,q_{j},q_{j})
, then

11.

\forall x\,((q_{i}(x)\wedge r_{\ell}(x)\wedge e_{\ell})\rightarrow(\exists^{=1}y\,R(x,y)\wedge\forall y\,(R(x,y)\rightarrow{}\\
\hskip 167.87108pt\exists^{=1}x\,R(x,y))))
,

12.

\forall x\,((q_{i}(x)\wedge r_{\ell}(x)\wedge\neg e_{\ell})\rightarrow(\exists^{=1}y\,R(x,y)\wedge\forall y\,(R(x,y)\rightarrow{}\\
\hskip 167.87108pt\exists^{=2}x\,R(x,y))))
,

13.

\forall x\,((q_{i}(x)\wedge\neg r_{\ell}(x))\rightarrow(\exists^{=1}y\,R(x,y)\wedge\forall y\,(R(x,y)\rightarrow{}\\
\hskip 167.87108pt\exists^{=1}x\,R(x,y))))
;

and finally

14.

\forall x\,((e_{\ell}(x)\wedge r_{\ell}(x))\rightarrow u(x))
.

Proposition 34
.

If
M
is non-halting, then there are pointed models
\mathfrak{A},x_{0}
and
\mathfrak{B},y_{0}
with
\mathfrak{A}\models\xi_{M}(x_{0})
and
\mathfrak{B}\models\chi_{M}(y_{0})

such that
\mathfrak{A},x_{0}\sim_{\mathsf{FO^{2}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},y_{0}
.

Proof.

The models in question are, as discussed above, two disjoint copies of the intended models of
\varphi_{M}
and
\psi_{M}
. It is straightforward to verify that these structures are indeed models of
\xi
and
\chi
, and that they are
\mathsf{FO^{2}}(\varrho)
-bisimilar.

\dashv

Next, the other direction:

Proposition 35
.

If there are pointed structures
\mathfrak{A},x_{0}
and
\mathfrak{B},y_{0}
such that
\mathfrak{A}\models\xi_{M}(x_{0})
,
\mathfrak{B}\models\chi_{M}(y_{0})

and
\mathfrak{A},x_{0}\sim_{\mathsf{FO^{2}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},y_{0}
,
then
M
is non-halting.

Proof.

This proof is mostly similar to that of Proposition
33
, except for the first few claims, in which we show certain elements to be bisimilar. We therefore prove the first few claims in detail, and refer to the proof of Proposition
33
for details on the remainder.

Let
\boldsymbol{\beta}
be the bisimulation between
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
, and let
A_{0}
be the elements in
\mathfrak{A}
that satisfy
q_{0}
and
B_{0}
the ones in
\mathfrak{B}
. Then, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
A_{n+1}
be the set of
R
-successors of elements of
A_{n}
and
B_{n+1}
the set of
R
-successors of
B_{n}
.

Claim 1.

\boldsymbol{\beta}
can only relate an element of
A_{n}
to elements of
B_{n}
, and vice versa.

Proof of Claim 1.
We proceed by induction. We show the base case for one direction, the other direction can be shown similarly. Suppose, towards a contradiction, that
(a,b)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
with
a\in A_{0}
and
b\not\in B_{0}
. Then, for any
x
,
(a,a)\mapsto(b,x)
is not a partial isomorphism, since
q_{0}(a)
but
\neg q_{0}(b)
. This contradicts
\boldsymbol{\beta}
being a bisimulation, so such
a
and
b
cannot exist.

Assume then, as induction hypothesis, that the claim holds for all
n^{\prime}<n
. Again, we show one direction: that
(a,b)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
a\in A_{n}
. The element
a
has an
a
-predecessor
a^{\prime}\in A_{n-1}
. Because
\boldsymbol{\beta}
is a bisimulation, there must be a
b^{\prime}
such that
(b,b^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
(a,a^{\prime})\mapsto(b,b^{\prime})
is a partial isomorphism. We have
R(a^{\prime},a)
, so we must also have
R(b^{\prime},b)
. Furthermore, by the induction hypothesis,
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
, together with
a^{\prime}\in A_{n-1}
, implies that
b^{\prime}\in B_{n-1}
. Hence
b
is the
R
-successor of an element in
B_{n-1}
, which, by definition, means
b\in B_{n}
. This completes the induction step, thereby proving the claim.

Claim 2.
If
a\in A_{n+1}
and
R(a^{\prime},a)
, then
a^{\prime}\in A_{n}
, and if
b\in B_{n+1}
and
R(b^{\prime},b)
, then
b^{\prime}\in B_{n}
.

Proof of Claim 2.
In
\mathfrak{A}
, every element, other than the two that satisfy
q_{0}
, has exactly one
R
-predecessor. So it immediately follows that every
R
-predecessor of
a\in A_{n+1}
is in
A_{n}
.
Now, take any
b\in B_{n+1}
and
b^{\prime}
such that
R(b^{\prime},b)
. There must be some
a
such that
(a,b)\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
. By Claim 1, this implies that
a\in A_{n+1}
. Furthermore, there is some
a^{\prime}
such that
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
(a^{\prime},a)\mapsto(b^{\prime},b)
is a partial isomorphism. As
R(b^{\prime},b)
, this implies that
R(a^{\prime},a)
as well, so we have
a^{\prime}\in A_{n}
. By Claim 1, this implies that
b^{\prime}\in B_{n}
.

Claim 3.
If
a_{0}\in A_{0}
and
b_{0}\in B_{0}
, then
(a_{0},b_{0})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
.

Proof of Claim 3.
Let
b_{0}
be one of the two elements of
B_{0}
. Then there is at least one
a_{0}\in A_{0}
such that
(a_{0},b_{0})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
. Let
a_{0}^{\prime}
be the other element of
A_{0}
. This
b_{0}
has exactly two
R
-successors
b_{1},b_{1}^{\prime}\in B_{1}
(with
b_{1}\not=b_{1}^{\prime}
). There must be
a_{1},a_{1}^{\prime}
such that
(a_{1},b_{1})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
,
(a_{1}^{\prime},b_{1}^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
, and
(a_{0},a_{1})\mapsto(b_{0},b_{1})
and
(a_{0},a_{1}^{\prime})\mapsto(b_{0},b_{1}^{\prime})
are partial isomorphisms. From
R(b_{0},b_{1})
and
R(b_{0},b_{1}^{\prime})
it therefore follows that
R(a_{0},a_{1})
and
R(a_{0},a_{0}^{\prime})
. As
a_{0}
has exactly one
R
-successor, this implies that
a_{0}=a_{0}^{\prime}
.

Now, consider the pair
(b_{1},b_{1}^{\prime})
. We have
(a_{1},b_{1})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
, so there must be some
a_{1}^{\prime\prime}
such that
(a_{1}^{\prime\prime},b_{1}^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
(a_{1},a_{1}^{\prime\prime})\mapsto(b_{1},b_{1}^{\prime})
is a partial isomorphism. This implies that, in particular,
a_{1}=a_{1}^{\prime\prime}
if and only if
b_{1}=b_{1}^{\prime}
. We have
b_{1}\not=b_{1}^{\prime}
, so
a_{1}\not=a_{1}^{\prime\prime}
. From
(a_{1}^{\prime\prime},b_{1}^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and Claim 1, it follows that
a_{1}^{\prime\prime}\in A_{1}
. Because
a_{1}\not=a_{1}^{\prime\prime}
, this
a_{1}^{\prime\prime}
must be the other element of
A_{1}
, which is the
R
-successor of
a_{0}^{\prime}
.

Because
(a_{1}^{\prime\prime},b_{1}^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
R(a_{0}^{\prime},a_{1}^{\prime\prime})
, there must be some
b_{0}^{\prime}
such that
(a_{0}^{\prime},b_{0}^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
R(b_{0}^{\prime},b_{1}^{\prime})
. But
b_{1}^{\prime}
is an
R
-successor of
b_{0}
and has only one
R
-predecessor, so
b_{0}^{\prime}=b_{0}^{\prime}
. Hence
(a_{0}^{\prime},b_{0}^{\prime})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
implies
(a_{0}^{\prime},b_{0})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
. We have now shown that, for arbitrary
b_{0}\in B_{0}
, we have
(a_{0},b_{0})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
(a_{0}^{\prime},b_{0})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
, with
a_{0}\not=a_{0}^{\prime}
. The claim follows immediately.

Claim 4.
If
a_{n}\in A_{n}
and
b_{n}\in B_{n}
, then
(a_{n},b_{n})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
.

Proof of Claim 4.
The proof is by induction. The base case is Claim 3. So assume as induction hypothesis that
n>0
and that Claim 4 holds for all
n^{\prime}<n
. Take any
a_{n}\in A_{n}
and
b_{n}\in B_{n}
. There are
a_{n-1}\in A_{n-1}
and
b_{n-1}\in B_{n-1}
such that
R(a_{n-1},a_{n})
and
R(b_{n-1},b_{n})
. By the induction hypothesis,
(a_{n-1},b_{n-1})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
. Now, there must be some
a_{n}^{\prime}
such that
(a_{n}^{\prime},b_{n})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
and
(a_{n-1},a_{n}^{\prime})\mapsto(b_{n-1},b_{n})
is a partial isomorphism. In particular, because
R(b_{n-1},b_{n})
, we have
R(a_{n-1},a_{n}^{\prime})
.

We already had
R(a_{n-1},a_{n})
, and
a_{n-1}
has only one
R
-successor, so
a_{n}=a_{n}^{\prime}
. As we have
(a_{n}^{\prime},b_{n})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
, this shows that
(a_{n},b_{n})\in\boldsymbol{\beta}
, thereby completing the induction step and therefore proving the claim.

The remainder of the proof proceeds in the same way as the proof of Proposition
33
.

\dashv

Thus, we have established that (Craig)
\mathsf{C^{2}}/\mathsf{FO^{2}}
-separation is RE-complete. It remains to show how the above proofs can be modified for the pairs
\mathsf{C^{2}}/L^{\prime}
, where
L^{\prime}
is any counting-free modal logic in Table
I
.
Because
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,n,u}}
can be viewed as a fragment of
\mathsf{C^{2}}
, it follows from the second item of Theorem
7
that (Craig)
\mathsf{C^{2}}/L^{\prime}
-separation is undecidable for all logics
L^{\prime}
with inverse. For modal logics without inverse, we can use the fact that
\mathsf{C^{2}}
can define inverse relations: we can add the conjunct
\forall x\,\forall y\,(R(x,y)\leftrightarrow T(y,x))
. Then, in models of the relevant formulas, any modal logic bisimulation will respect the relation
T
in addition to the relation
R
, and therefore also respect inverses. This completes the proof of Theorem
7
.

See

Proof.

First, we show the upper bounds. By Lemma
5
, it suffices to consider Craig separation. Let
\varphi_{1}
,
\varphi_{2}
be
\mathsf{GML\!^{n,u}}
-formulas,
\sigma=\mathit{sig}(\varphi_{1})\cup\mathit{sig}(\varphi_{2})
and
\varrho=\mathit{sig}(\varphi_{1})\cap\mathit{sig}(\varphi_{2})
. Denote by

the maximal number
k
such that
\Diamond_{R}^{\geq k}

\varphi_{1}
or
\varphi_{2}
, for some

.
A

(
for
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
) is any set
\mathfrak{t}\subseteq\textit{sub}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
, for which there is a pointed
\sigma
-structure
\mathfrak{A},a
such that
\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}(\mathfrak{A},a)

\mathfrak{t}(\mathfrak{A},a)=\{\chi\in\textit{sub}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\mid\mathfrak{A}\models\chi(a)\}

realised by

in
\mathfrak{A}

|\boldsymbol{T}|\leq 2^{|\varphi_{1}|+|\varphi_{2}|}

\boldsymbol{T}
can be computed in time exponential in
|\varphi_{1}|+|\varphi_{2}|
.
Types
\mathfrak{t}_{1}

\Diamondblack
-equivalent

\Diamondblack\chi\in\mathfrak{t}_{2}
, for all
\Diamondblack\chi\in\textit{sub}(\varphi,\psi)
. Note that the types realised in the same structure are
\Diamondblack
-equivalent to each other.

We check the existence of
\sigma
-structures

and
\mathfrak{A},a_{2}

\mathfrak{A}_{i}\models\varphi_{i}(a_{i})
,

, and

using a mosaic-elimination procedure, a generalisation of the standard type-elimination procedure for satisfiability-checking; see, e.g.,
[

.

A

(
for
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
) is a pair

of non-empty sets

of types such that the following hold:

–

iff
A\in\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
, for all

and
\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in M_{1}\cup M_{2}
;

–

N_{c}\in\mathfrak{t}
iff
N_{c}\in\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
, for all

and
\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in M_{1}\cup M_{2}
;

–

if
\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in M_{i}
,

,
N_{c}\in\mathfrak{t}\cap\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
, then
\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
, for all
c\in\sigma
;

–

all types in each
M_{i}
are
\Diamondblack
-equivalent to each other.

We say that
N_{c}

occurs

if
N_{c}\in\mathfrak{t}\in M_{i}
, for some
\mathfrak{t}
and

.
As follows from the second and third items, if

occurs in
M
, for some

and

are singletons.

There are at most doubly exponentially many mosaics, and they can be computed in double exponential time. Let
f
be the function defined by taking

if

contains a type with a nominal

otherwise.

The definition of mosaic above is an abstraction from the following semantical notion. Given structures
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{A}_{2}
, the

mosaic defined by
a\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{i})
in
\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2}
,

Clearly,

is a mosaic and
M(\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2},a)=M(\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2},a^{\prime})
if

, for any
a,a^{\prime}\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{1})\cup{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{2})
.

We aim to build the required structures
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
,
i=1,2
, and bisimulation
\boldsymbol{\beta}
between them—if this is at all possible—from copies of suitable pairs

with a mosaic

and a type

.
To satisfy the graded modalities

in the
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
, we require the following definition.
Let

be a mosaic,

contain a formula

. We call

R
-witness for

if there exists a relation

with

\displaystyle\Delta_{i}=\{(\mathfrak{t},M)\mid\mathfrak{t}\in M_{i}\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i}=\{(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},M^{\prime},j)\mid\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in M_{i}^{\prime},\ M^{\prime}=(M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{M},\ 1\leq j\leq f(M^{\prime})\}

satisfying the following for

and

\Diamondblack
-harmony)

\Diamondblack

;

(witness)

for any

and
(\mathfrak{t},M)\in\Delta
, we have

\mbox{}\hskip-19.91692pt\Diamond^{\geq k}_{R}\chi\in\mathfrak{t}\text{ iff }\big|\{(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},M^{\prime},j)\in\Gamma\mid\chi\in\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},(\mathfrak{t},M)R^{M,\mathcal{M}}(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},M^{\prime},j)\}\big|\geq k;

(bisim)

if
R\in\varrho
,
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
, and
(\mathfrak{t}_{0},M)\in\Delta
has an
R^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-successor

-successor of the form

.

The intuition behind conditions

It should also be clear that if

We can now define the mosaic elimination procedure for a set

of mosaics. Call

Lemma 36
.

Given a set
\mathfrak{S}
of mosaics and
M\in\mathfrak{S}
, it can be decided in double exponential time in

whether

is bad in
\mathfrak{S}
.

Proof.

There are at most doubly exponentially-many candidate sets
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}
with
|\mathcal{M}|\leq k^{\bullet}2^{|\varphi_{1}|+|\varphi_{2}|}
and binary relations
R^{M,\mathcal{M}}\subseteq(\Delta_{1}\times\Gamma_{1})\cup(\Delta_{2}\times\Gamma_{2})
. We simply check that none of the pairs
\mathcal{M}
,
R^{M,\mathcal{M}}
satisfies
(
\Diamondblack
-harmony)
,
(witness)
and
(bisim)
.

\dashv

Let
\mathfrak{S}_{0}
be the set of all mosaics. Let
h
be a function that, for each pair
c\in\sigma
and
i\in\{1,2\}
, selects one mosaic
h(N_{c},i)=(M_{1},M_{2})
such that

–

N_{c}\in\mathfrak{t}
, for some
\mathfrak{t}\in M_{i}
;

–

if

, for some
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in M_{j}
,

and

h(N_{c},i)=h(N_{c^{\prime}},j)
.

By the definition of mosaics,
h(N_{c},1)=h(N_{c},2)
for all
c\in\varrho
.

Given such
h
, let
\mathfrak{S}_{0}^{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{0}
comprise all mosaics of the form

and those that do not have occurrences of nominals. Starting from
\mathfrak{S}_{0}^{h}
, we construct inductively sets
\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}^{h}
,

, by looking through the mosaics in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{h}
and eliminating the bad ones. We stop when
\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}^{h}=\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{h}
and set
\mathfrak{S}_{\ast}^{h}=\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{h}
. By Lemma
36
,
\mathfrak{S}_{\ast}^{h}
can be constructed in double exponential time.

\dashv

This lemma in tandem with Lemma
