give a
2\textsc{ExpTime}
upper bound for deciding (Craig)
\mathsf{GML\!^{n,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{n,u}}
-separation. We can easily modify the construction to establish the same upper bound for
\mathsf{GML\!^{n,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{n}}
,
\mathsf{GML\!^{n,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{u}}
,
\mathsf{GML\!^{u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{u}}
, and
\mathsf{GML\!^{u}}/\mathsf{ML}
:

–

For
\mathsf{GML\!^{n,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{n}}
, we require that
\mathfrak{A}_{1},a_{1}\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{n}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{A}_{2},a_{2}
in Lemma
9

(i)
. To this end we admit mosaics
(M_{1},M_{2})
in which
M_{1}
or
M_{2}
can be empty (but not both). Observe that now
h(N_{c},1)=h(N_{c},2)
does not necessarily hold for all
c\in\varrho
since the second component of
h(N_{c},1)
and the first component of
h(N_{c},2)
can be empty, reflecting the fact that
\mathfrak{A}_{1},c^{\mathfrak{A}_{1}}
is not necessarily
\mathsf{ML\!^{n}}(\varrho)
-bisimilar to
\mathfrak{A}_{2},c^{\mathfrak{A}_{2}}
if
c\in\varrho
and
\mathfrak{A}_{1},a_{1}\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{n}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{A}_{2},a_{2}
.

–

For
\mathsf{GML\!^{n,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{u}}
, we require that
\mathfrak{A}_{1},a_{1}\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{u}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{A}_{2},a_{2}
in Lemma
9

(i)
. To this end, given
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
, we replace all
N_{c}
in
\varphi_{2}
, for
c\in\mathit{sig}(\varphi_{1})\cap\mathit{sig}(\varphi_{2})
, by fresh
N_{c^{\prime}}
and apply the algorithm above to
\varphi_{1}
and the resulting formula
\varphi_{2}^{\prime}
.

–

For
\mathsf{GML\!^{u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{u}}
, we omit everything related to nominals.

–

For
\mathsf{GML\!^{u}}/\mathsf{ML}
, we omit everything related to nominals and allow mosaics with one empty component.

A somewhat different argument is required to establish the
coNExpTime
upper bounds of Theorem
8
for
\mathsf{GML\!^{n}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{n}}
- and
\mathsf{GML}/\mathsf{ML}
-separation in the
\Diamondblack
-free languages.
First, we again ignore everything related to
\Diamondblack
and admit mosaics
M=(M_{1},M_{2})
with
M_{1}=\emptyset
or
M_{2}=\emptyset
(but not both). Second, we observe that, without
\Diamondblack
, we can significantly simplify the structures
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
,
i=1,2
, from the proof of Lemma
9

(ii)\Rightarrow(i)
. Let
m=\max\{\textit{md}(\varphi_{1}),\textit{md}(\varphi_{2})\}
, where
\textit{md}(\varphi_{i})
is the
modal depth
of
\varphi_{i}
, i.e., the maximal number of nested modal operators in
\varphi_{i}
. Let
{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}_{|m}
be the restrictions of the
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
to words
s
of the form (
4
) such that

–

n\leq m
and no pair from
R^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}
starts at
s
of length
m
;

–

in the first triple
(\mathfrak{t}_{0},M_{0},i_{0})
of
s
, either
\mathfrak{t}_{0}\in\{\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{1},\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{2}\}
and
M_{0}=M^{*}
or
h(N_{c},i)=M_{0}
, for some
c\in\sigma
.

Thus, the size of the
{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}_{|m}
is (singly) exponential in
|\varphi_{1}|+|\varphi_{2}|
.
As well-known from modal logic
[
54
,
55
]
,
{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}_{|m}\models\varphi_{i}(\mathfrak{t}_{i}^{\ast},M^{\ast})
, for
i=1,2
. Moreover,
the restriction of
\boldsymbol{\beta}
to
{\mathfrak{A}_{1}}_{|m}\times{\mathfrak{A}_{2}}_{|m}
is still an
\mathsf{ML\!^{n}}(\varrho)
-bisimulation, and so
{\mathfrak{A}_{1}}_{|m},(\mathfrak{t}_{1}^{\ast},M^{\ast},1)\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{n}}(\varrho)}{\mathfrak{A}_{2}}_{|m},(\mathfrak{t}_{2}^{\ast},M^{\ast},1)
. The existence of such structures
{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}_{|m}
can clearly be checked in
NExpTime
.
In the case of
\mathsf{GML}/\mathsf{ML}
-separation, we ignore everything related to nominals.

We now prove the matching 2
ExpTime
and
co
NExpTime
lower bounds. By Lemma
5
, it suffices to do this for unary Craig separation.
By Lemma
4
, we need to show 2
ExpTime
-hardness of the following problem for
L^{\prime}\in\{\mathsf{ML\!^{u}},\mathsf{ML}\}
: given
\mathsf{GML\!^{u}}
-formulas
\varphi
and
\psi
, decide whether there exist pointed structures
\mathfrak{A},a
and
\mathfrak{B},b
such that
\mathfrak{A}\models\varphi(a)
,
\mathfrak{B}\models\psi(b)
, and
\mathfrak{A},a\sim_{L^{\prime}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},b
, where
\varrho
is the set of all binary predicates
R\in\mathit{sig}(\varphi)\cup\mathit{sig}(\psi)
and all propositional variables
A\in\mathit{sig}(\varphi)\cap\mathit{sig}(\psi)
. This also implies 2
ExpTime
-hardness for the relevant logics with nominals.
To establish the
co
NExpTime
lower bound, it suffices to show
NExpTime
-hardness of the same problem for
\mathsf{GML}
-formulas
\varphi
,
\psi
and
L^{\prime}=\mathsf{ML}
.

Both proofs are similar to the lower bound proofs for Craig interpolant existence in description logics with nominals and role inclusions given in
[
29
, Sections 7, 9]
. The underlying idea is to construct formulas
\varphi_{n}
and
\psi_{n}
, for
n<\omega
, in the required language and of polynomial size in
n
such that if
\mathfrak{A}\models\varphi_{n}(a)
,
\mathfrak{B}\models\psi_{n}(b)
, and
\mathfrak{A},a\sim_{L^{\prime}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},b
, then there is a set
E\subseteq{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{B})

of size
2^{n}
with the following properties:

–

all
e\in E
are
L^{\prime}(\varrho)
-bisimilar, and

–

the
e\in E
satisfy all pairwise distinct combinations of variables
A_{0},\ldots,A_{n-1}
(which cannot be in
\varrho
).

We show how to do this for
L=\mathsf{GML}
and
L^{\prime}\in\{\mathsf{ML\!^{u}},\mathsf{ML}\}
. The formula

\varphi_{n}=\Diamond_{R}^{=1}\dots\Diamond_{R}^{=1}\top
with
n
-many
\Diamond_{R}^{=1}
is such that
\mathfrak{A}\models\varphi_{n}(a)
iff
\mathfrak{A}
has a unique
R
-chain of length
n
starting from
a
. The formula
\psi_{n}
is defined as the conjunction of

–

\Box_{R}^{i}\Diamond_{R}^{=2}\top
, for
0\leq i<n
;

–

\Box_{R}^{i}(\Diamond_{R}A_{i}\wedge\Diamond_{R}\neg A_{i})
, for
0\leq i<n
;

–

\Box_{R}^{i}\big((A_{j}\rightarrow\Box_{R}A_{j})\wedge(\neg A_{j}\rightarrow\Box_{R}\neg A_{j})\big)
, for
0\leq j<i<n
.

We have
\mathfrak{B}\models\psi_{n}(b)
iff the
R
-chains of depth
\leq n
starting from
b
comprise a binary
R
-tree of depth
n
with leaves satisfying counter values from
0
to
2^{n}-1
represented by the variables
A_{0},\dots,A_{n-1}
. So, if
\mathfrak{A}\models\varphi_{n}(a)
,
\mathfrak{B}\models\psi_{n}(b)
, and
\mathfrak{A},a\sim_{\mathsf{ML}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},b
, then there is a unique
a^{\prime}\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})
reachable via an
R
-chain of length
n
from
a
, and there are leaves
E=\{e_{0},\dots,e_{2^{n}-1}\}
of the binary tree with root
b
in
\mathfrak{B}
such that
e_{i}
satisfies the binary encoding of
i
by means of
A_{0},\dots,A_{n-1}
, and so

\mathfrak{A},{a^{\prime}}\sim_{L^{\prime}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},e_{0}\sim_{L^{\prime}(\varrho)}\cdots\sim_{L^{\prime}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{B},e_{2^{n}-1}
by the definition of
L^{\prime}(\varrho)
-bisimulation and
R\in\varrho
.

For the 2
ExpTime
-lower bound, the nodes in
E
can then be used to encode
the acceptance problem for exponential space-bounded alternating Turing machines (ATMs). Roughly, the idea is to start from each
e\in E
a simulation of the computation tree of the ATM for an input word of length
n
. On the shared symbols in
\varrho
, these trees are essentially isomorphic. Using the variables
A_{0},\dots,A_{n-1}
as bits, one can create, for each
m<2^{n}
on some
e\in E
,
a
2^{n}
-yardstick starting at
m
and coordinating the labels of the computation tree at positions
m+l2^{n}
,
l\geq 0
. This is also achieved using counters encoded by variables that are not in
\varrho
. As one has to look ‘exponentially deep’ into the computation tree, the universal modality
\Diamondblack
of
\mathsf{GML\!^{u}}
is required when writing up the encoding; see
[
29
]
for details.

For the
NExpTime
-lower bound, the nodes in
E
can be used to encode
a
2^{n}\times 2^{n}
-torus tiling problem. In this case, assume for simplicity that
|E|=2^{2n}
. Then, for each grid cell
(i,j)
with
i,j<2^{n}
, we use some
e_{i,j}\in E
satisfying the encoding of
i
via
A_{0},\dots,A_{n-1}
and that of
j
via
A_{n},\dots,A_{2n-1}
.
Using successors of the nodes in
E
(which coincide on
\varrho
, for each
e\in E
),
one can ensure the horizontal and vertical matching conditions of the tiling problem.

\dashv

Proofs for Section
VI

See
10

Proof.

We start by establishing the upper bound and focusing on Craig separation in view of Lemma
5
. Consider first
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,u}}/\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}
. Let
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
be some
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,u}}
-formulas,
\sigma=\mathit{sig}(\varphi_{1})\cup\mathit{sig}(\varphi_{2})
and
\varrho=\mathit{sig}(\varphi_{1})\cap\mathit{sig}(\varphi_{2})
. We employ the notion of type introduced in Section
V
and adapted in the obvious way for
\mathsf{GML\!^{i,u}}
. We need to check whether there are pointed
\sigma
-structures
\mathfrak{A}_{1},a_{1}
and
\mathfrak{A}_{2},a_{2}
, and a
\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}(\varrho)
-bisimulation
\boldsymbol{\beta}
between
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{A}_{2}
such that
\mathfrak{A}_{1}\models\varphi_{1}(a_{1})
,
\mathfrak{A}_{2}\models\varphi_{2}(a_{2})
and
a_{1}\boldsymbol{\beta}a_{2}
.

We construct
\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2}
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}
(if they exist) from mosaics, which are appropriately modified to deal with inverse modalities. To this end, we supply each node with some information about the types of its ‘parents and children’​. Following
[
12
, Sec. 8.3]
, we call the result a star-type. To give a precise definition, we require some notations.

The
neighbourhood

N(\mathfrak{A},a)
of a node
a\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})
in a
\sigma
-structure
\mathfrak{A}
is the set

N(\mathfrak{A},a)=\{b\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})\mid(a,b)\in S^{\mathfrak{A}},\ S\in\{R,R^{-}\},\ R\in\sigma\}.

A
successor type set

\mathfrak{s}
(
for
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
) is a set containing, for each type
\mathfrak{t}
and each
S
, exactly one expression of the form
\Diamond_{S}^{=n}\mathfrak{t}
, for
0\leq n\leq k^{\bullet}
, or
\Diamond_{S}^{>k^{\bullet}}\mathfrak{t}
. Let
a\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})
,
D\subseteq{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})
, and
n(a,S,\mathfrak{t},D)=|\{b\in D\mid(a,b)\in S^{\mathfrak{A}},\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}(\mathfrak{A},b)\}|
. The successor type set
\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{A},a,D)
defined by
a
and
D
contains

–

\Diamond_{S}^{=n}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{A},a,D)
if
n=n(a,S,\mathfrak{t},D)\leq k^{\bullet}
, and

–

\Diamond_{S}^{>k^{\bullet}}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{A},a,D)
if
n(a,S,\mathfrak{t},D)>k^{\bullet}
.

Observe that
\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{A},a,D)=\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{A},a,D\cap N(\mathfrak{A},a))
, so only nodes in the neighbourhood of
a
are relevant for the definition of
\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{A},a,D)
.
Occasionally, we are only interested in the existence of an
S
-successor in
D
with a type
\mathfrak{t}
, writing
\Diamond_{S}^{>0}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}
as a shorthand for ‘
\Diamond_{S}^{=n}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}

for some
n
,
1\leq n\leq k^{\bullet}
, or
\Diamond_{S}^{>k^{\bullet}}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}
’​. We omit
\mathfrak{A}
from
\mathfrak{s}(\mathfrak{A},a,D)
,
\mathfrak{t}(\mathfrak{A},a)
,
N(\mathfrak{A},a)
, etc., if understood. We also omit elements of the form
\Diamond^{=0}_{S}\mathfrak{t}
from successor type sets.

We define a
star-type
as a triple of the form
\mathfrak{st}=(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})
, where
\mathfrak{t}
is a type,
\mathfrak{s}_{p}
a
parent
successor type set, and
\mathfrak{s}_{c}
a
child
successor type set. To be coherent with the semantics of graded modalities,
\mathfrak{st}
should satisfy some natural conditions defined below.
The
S
-profile
P_{S}(\mathfrak{s})
of
\mathfrak{s}\in\{\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c}\}
contains, for any
\chi\in\textit{sub}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
,

–

\Diamond_{S}^{=n}\chi
if
n
is the sum over all
k
with
\Diamond_{S}^{=k}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}
and
\chi\in\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
;

–

\Diamond_{S}^{>k^{\bullet}}\chi
if this sum exceeds
k^{\bullet}
or
\Diamond_{S}^{>k^{\bullet}}t^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}
, for some
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\ni\chi
.

The
S
-profile
P_{S}(\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})
of
\mathfrak{s}_{p}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{c}
contains, for all formulas
\chi\in\textit{sub}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
,

–

\Diamond_{S}^{=n}\chi
if
n=n_{1}+n_{2}\leq k^{\bullet}
for
\Diamond_{S}^{=n_{1}}\chi
in the
S
-profile of
\mathfrak{s}_{p}
and
\Diamond_{S}^{=n_{2}}\chi
in the
S
-profile of
\mathfrak{s}_{c}
;

–

\Diamond_{S}^{>k^{\bullet}}\chi
otherwise.

A star-type
\mathfrak{st}=(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})
is called
coherent
if, for all formulas
\Diamond_{S}^{\geq n}\chi\in\textit{sub}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
, we have
\Diamond_{S}^{\geq n}\chi\in\mathfrak{t}
iff
P_{S}(\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})
contains either
\Diamond_{S}^{=n^{\prime}}\chi
, for
some
n^{\prime}\geq n
, or
\Diamond_{S}^{>k^{\bullet}}\chi
. From now on, all star-types we consider are assumed to be coherent.

The
star-type realised by
a\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})
and
D\subseteq{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})
is defined as

\mathfrak{st}(a,D)=\big(\mathfrak{t}(a),\mathfrak{s}(a,D),\mathfrak{s}(a,{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})\setminus D)\big).

Instead of
{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A})\setminus D
, we can take
N(a)\setminus D
.

A
mosaic
is a pair
M=(M_{1},M_{2})
of sets of star-types such that

–

A\in\mathfrak{t}
iff
A\in\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
, for all
(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c}),(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime})\in M_{1}\cup M_{2}
and
A\in\varrho
;

–

for any
(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})\in M_{1}\cup M_{2}
, all types in
\mathfrak{s}_{p}\cup\mathfrak{s}_{c}
are
\Diamondblack
-equivalent to
\mathfrak{t}
.

Given structures
\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2}
with
a\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{j_{1}})
and
p\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{j_{2}})
, for some
j_{1},j_{2}\in\{1,2\}
, the
mosaic defined by
a
and
p
is

M(\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2},a,p)=\big(M_{1}(\mathfrak{A}_{1},a,p),M_{2}(\mathfrak{A}_{2},a,p)\big),

where, for
D_{i}=\{d\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{i})\mid\mathfrak{A}_{i},d\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{A}_{j_{2}},p\}
and
i=1,2
,

M_{i}(\mathfrak{A}_{i},a,p)=\{\mathfrak{st}(\mathfrak{A}_{i},e,D_{i})\mid{}\\
e\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{i}),\mathfrak{A}_{i},e\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{A}_{j_{1}},a\}.

We are also interested in
root mosaics

M
with
\Diamond_{S}^{=0}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}_{p}
, for all
(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})\in M_{1}\cup M_{2}
, all
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
and all
S
. We obtain a root mosaic
M(\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2},a,\emptyset)=(M_{1}(\mathfrak{A}_{1},a,\emptyset),M_{2}(\mathfrak{A}_{2},a,\emptyset))
with
a\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{j})
and
j\in\{1,2\}
by taking, for
i=1,2
,

M_{i}(\mathfrak{A}_{i},a,\emptyset)=\{\mathfrak{st}(\mathfrak{A}_{i},e,\emptyset)\mid e\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{i}),\mathfrak{A}_{i},e\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{A}_{j},a\}.

As in Section
V
, we are going to use mosaics for building the structures
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{A}_{2}
we are after. To this end, we require the following definition. Let
M=(M_{1},M_{2})
be a mosaic and
\mathcal{M}
a set of mosaics. Set
\Delta=\Delta_{1}\cup\Delta_{2}
and
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}
, where, for
i=1,2
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{i}=\{(\mathfrak{st},j)\mid j<\omega,\ \mathfrak{st}\in M_{i}\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i}=\{(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},j^{\prime},M^{\prime})\mid j<\omega,\ \mathfrak{st}^{\prime}\in M_{i}^{\prime},\ M^{\prime}=(M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{M}\}.

Intuitively,
\Delta_{i}
consists of
\omega
-many copies of each star-type in
M_{i}
, and
\Gamma_{i}
of
\omega
-many copies of each ‘pointed mosaic’
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{M}
.

We call
\mathcal{M}
a
witness
for
M
if, for each
S
, there is a relation

S^{M,\mathcal{M}}\subseteq(\Delta_{1}\times\Gamma_{1})\cup(\Delta_{2}\cup\Gamma_{2})

such that the following conditions are satisfied:

(witness
1
)

if
(\mathfrak{st},j)\in\Delta
,
\mathfrak{st}=(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})
and
\Diamond_{S}^{=n}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}_{c}
, then
(\mathfrak{st},j)
has exactly
n
different
S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-successors of type
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
—i.e., of the form
((\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime}),j^{\prime},M^{\prime})
—in
\Gamma
; if
\Diamond_{S}^{>k^{\bullet}}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}_{c}
, then
(\mathfrak{st},j)
has at least
k^{\bullet}+1
different
S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-successors of type
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
in
\Gamma
;

(witness
2
)

if
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},k,M^{\prime})\in\Gamma
,
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime})
and
\Diamond_{S}^{=n}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime}
, then
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},k,M^{\prime})
has exactly
n

S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-successors of type
\mathfrak{t}
in
\Delta
; if
\Diamond_{S}^{>k^{\bullet}}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime}
, then
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},k,M^{\prime})
has
>k^{\bullet}

S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-successors of type
\mathfrak{t}
in
\Delta
;

(bisim
1
)

if
R\in\varrho
,
S\in\{R,R^{-}\}
and some element of
\Delta
has an
S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-successor
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},j^{\prime},M^{\prime})\in\Gamma
, then every element of
\Delta
has an
S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-successor of the form
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime},M^{\prime})\in\Gamma
;

(bisim
2
)

if
R\in\varrho
,
S\in\{R,R^{-}\}
and some
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},j^{\prime},M^{\prime})\in\Gamma
is an
S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-successor of an element of
\Delta
, then all
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime},M^{\prime})
in
\Gamma
are
S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-successors of some element of
\Delta
.

Our aim is to show that there exist pointed structures
\mathfrak{A}_{i},a_{i}
,
i=1,2
, satisfying
\varphi_{i}
with
\mathfrak{A}_{1},a_{1}\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{A}_{2},a_{2}
iff there exists a set
\mathfrak{S}
of mosaics such that

(m1)

if
M=(M_{1},M_{2})\in\mathfrak{S}
, then
M_{1}\not=\emptyset
and
M_{2}\not=\emptyset
;

(m2)

there are a root mosaic
M=(M_{1},M_{2})\in\mathfrak{S}
and star-types
\mathfrak{st}_{i}\in M_{i}
containing
\varphi_{i}
in their types, for
i=1,2
;

(m3)

every
M\in\mathfrak{S}
has a witness
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}
;

(m4)

if
\Diamondblack\chi\in\mathfrak{t}
for some
(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})\in M_{i}
and
M=(M_{1},M_{2})\in\mathfrak{S}
, then there exists a root mosaic
M^{\prime}=(M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{S}
such that
\chi\in\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
for some
(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime})\in M_{i}^{\prime}
such that
\mathfrak{t}
and
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
are
\Diamondblack
-equivalent, for
i=1,2
.

Moreover, we also aim to show that it suffices to look for
\mathfrak{S}
with mosaics and witnesses of exponential size, which would make it possible to check the existence of such an
\mathfrak{S}
in double exponential size. With this in mind, we first give a somewhat different, but equivalent ‘syntactic’ definition of witnesses.

We say that star-types
\mathfrak{st}=(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})
and
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime})
are
S
-coherent
and write
\mathfrak{st}\rightsquigarrow_{S}\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}
if
\Diamond_{S}^{>0}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}_{c}
and
\Diamond_{S^{-}}^{>0}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}_{p}
.
Let
M=(M_{1},M_{2})
be a mosaic,
\mathcal{M}
a set of mosaics and

\mathcal{M}_{i}^{\bullet}=\{(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime})\mid M^{\prime}=(M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{M},\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}\in M_{i}^{\prime}\},\quad i=1,2.

Call
\mathcal{M}
is a
syntactic witness
for
M
if, for every
S
, there is a relation

\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}\subseteq(M_{1}\times\mathcal{M}_{1}^{\bullet})\cup(M_{2}\times\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\bullet})

satisfying the following conditions, for
i=1,2
:

(coherence)

if
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
, then
\mathfrak{st}\rightsquigarrow_{S}\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}
;

(bisim)

if
R\in\varrho
,
S\in\{R,R^{-}\}
, and
M^{\prime}=(M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{M}
, then either

–

for any
\mathfrak{st}\in M_{i}
, there is
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}\in M_{i}^{\prime}
with
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
and, for any
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}\in M_{i}^{\prime}
, there is
\mathfrak{st}\in M_{i}
with
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
,

in which case
M^{\prime}
is called an
S
-successor of
M
,
or

–

(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\notin\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
for any
\mathfrak{st}\in M_{i}
and
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}\in M_{i}^{\prime}
;

(wit
1
)

if
\mathfrak{st}=(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})\in M_{i}
and
\Diamond_{S}^{>0}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}_{c}
, then there are
M^{\prime}=(M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{M}
and

\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime})\in M_{i}^{\prime}
such that

(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
;

(wit
2
)

if
M^{\prime}=(M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{M}
,
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime})\in M_{i}^{\prime}
and
\Diamond_{S^{-}}^{>0}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime}
, then there is
\mathfrak{st}=(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})\in M_{i}
with
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
;

(wit
3
)

if
R\in\varrho
,
S\in\{R,R^{-}\}
and
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
, where
\mathfrak{st}=(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})\in M_{i}
, then there is a function
g
that assigns to each
S
-successor
M^{*}=(M_{1}^{*},M_{2}^{*})
of
M
in
\mathcal{M}
a star-type in
M_{i}^{*}
such that
g(M^{\prime})=\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}
and, for any
\Diamond_{S}^{=n}\mathfrak{t}_{1}\in\mathfrak{s}_{c}
, we have

|\{M^{*}\in\mathcal{M}\mid g(M^{*})=(\mathfrak{t}_{1},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{*},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{*}),\text{ for some }\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{*},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{*}\}|\leq n.

The intuition behind the concept of a syntactic witness is as follows. If
\mathcal{M}
is a witness for
M
, then for every
\mathfrak{st}=(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})\in M_{1}\cup M_{2}
, if
\Diamond^{=n}_{S}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}_{c}
then we require
(\mathfrak{st},j)
to be
S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-related to exactly
n
different star-types based on type
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
. A similar constraint holds for
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime})\in M_{1}^{\prime}\cup M_{2}^{\prime}
, where
(M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{M}
. For a syntactic witness, we relax this requirement: if
\Diamond^{=n}_{S}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}_{c}
for some
n>0
we do require
\mathfrak{st}
to be
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-related to at least one star-type that is based on
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
, but we do not require there to be exactly
n
. The reason for this relaxation is that we can create multiple copies of each star-type: if, say,
\Diamond^{=2}_{S}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}_{c}
and
\mathfrak{st}
is
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-related to exactly one
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime})
, where
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}
is based on type
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
, then every
(\mathfrak{st},j)
will be
S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-related to
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},j_{1}^{\prime},M^{\prime})
and
(\mathfrak{st},j_{2}^{\prime},M^{\prime})
with
j_{1}^{\prime}\neq j_{2}^{\prime}
.

Similarly, if
\Diamond^{=1}_{S}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}_{c}
and
\mathfrak{st}
is
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-related to both
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime})
and
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime},M^{\prime\prime})
, where
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime}
are based on type
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
, then there will be different
j_{1}
and
j_{2}
such that
(\mathfrak{st},j_{1})
is
S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-related to
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},j^{\prime},M^{\prime})
and
(\mathfrak{st},j_{2})
is
s^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-related to
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime},M^{\prime\prime})
.

Such copying does, of course, require there to be at least one
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-successor when
\Diamond^{>0}_{S}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}_{c}
. This is enforced by
(wit
1
)
, while
(wit
2
)
is the corresponding requirement for
\Diamond^{>0}_{S}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime}
.

There is one other requirement, however, that is slightly harder to explain. Suppose that
\Diamond^{=1}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}_{c}
and that
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}_{1}^{\prime},M^{\prime})),(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}_{2}^{\prime},M^{\prime\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
, where
\mathfrak{st}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{st}_{2}^{\prime}
are both based on
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime}\neq M^{\prime\prime}
. Suppose furthermore that
\mathfrak{st}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{st}_{2}^{\prime}
are the only
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-successors of
\mathfrak{st}
in their respective mosaics. If we were to try to create a witness
S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
from this
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
, we would take
((\mathfrak{st},j),(\mathfrak{st}_{1}^{\prime},j_{1}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
and
((\mathfrak{st},j),(\mathfrak{st}_{2}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime},M^{\prime\prime}))\in S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
. But there would be no
\mathfrak{st}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
and
j_{2}^{\prime\prime}
such that
((\mathfrak{st},j),(\mathfrak{st}_{2}^{\prime\prime},j_{2}^{\prime\prime},M^{\prime\prime}))\in S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
and no
\mathfrak{st}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
j_{1}^{\prime\prime}
such that
((\mathfrak{st},j),(\mathfrak{st}_{1}^{\prime\prime},j_{1}^{\prime\prime},M^{\prime}))\in S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
. Therefore, the
(bisim
1
)
condition would not be satisfied.

In order to prevent this kind of situation, we must ensure that if
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
, then there is some way to choose
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}
as a successor of
\mathfrak{st}
that respects both graded modalities and bisimulation restrictions. This means it must be possible to choose one
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime}
per
S
-successor
M^{\prime\prime}
of
M
such that (i) for the mosaic
M^{\prime}
we choose
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}
and (ii) if
\Diamond^{=n}_{S}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}_{c}
then we choose at most
n
star-types based on type
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime\prime}
. In our definition of a syntactic witness, this requirement is included as
(wit
3
)
.

Now that we have explained the intuition behind syntactic witnesses, we can show that syntactic witnesses are witnesses, and vice versa.

Lemma 37
.

\mathcal{M}
is a witness for
M
iff
\mathcal{M}
is a syntactic witness for
M
.

Proof.

(\Rightarrow)
Suppose
\mathcal{M}
is a witness for
M=(M_{1},M_{2})
and, for each
S
, let
S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
be the witnessing relation with respect to
S
. Let
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
be the relation given by
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime})\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
iff there are
j,j^{\prime}
such that
((\mathfrak{st},j),(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},j^{\prime},N^{\prime}))\in S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
.

We show that
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
satisfies the conditions for
\mathcal{M}
being a syntactic witness for
M
.

(coherence)

Suppose that
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
, where
\mathfrak{st}=(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})
and
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime})
. Then, by the definition of
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
, there are
j,j^{\prime}
such that
((\mathfrak{st},j),(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},j^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
.

By the
(witness
1
)
condition,
((\mathfrak{st},j),(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},j^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
implies that
\Diamond^{=0}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\not\in\mathfrak{s}_{c}
, and hence that
\Diamond^{>0}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}_{c}
. Similarly, by
(witness
2
)
, it implies that
\Diamond^{=0}_{S^{-}}\mathfrak{t}\not\in\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}_{p}
, and hence
\Diamond^{>0}_{S^{-}}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}_{p}
. This shows that
\mathfrak{st}\rightsquigarrow_{S}\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}
, so the
(coherence)
condition is satisfied.

(bisim)

Consider any
M^{\prime}=(M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{M}
and any
i\in\{1,2\}
. Assume
R\in\varrho
and
S\in\{R,R^{-}\}
. We distinguish two cases. As a first case, suppose that there are
\mathfrak{st}_{1}\in M_{i}
,
\mathfrak{st}_{1}^{\prime}\in M_{i}^{\prime}
and
j_{1},j_{1}^{\prime}<\omega
such that
((\mathfrak{st}_{1},j_{1}),(\mathfrak{st}_{1}^{\prime},j_{1}^{\prime},M_{i}^{\prime})\in S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
. Then
(bisim
1
)
implies that for every
\mathfrak{st}_{2}\in M_{i}
and every
j_{2}<\omega
there are
\mathfrak{st}_{2}^{\prime}\in M_{i}^{\prime}
and
j_{2}^{\prime}<\omega
such that
((\mathfrak{st}_{2},j_{2}),(\mathfrak{st}_{2}^{\prime},j_{2}^{\prime},M^{\prime})\in S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
.

So, for every such
\mathfrak{st}_{2}
, there is a
\mathfrak{st}_{2}^{\prime}
such that
(\mathfrak{st}_{2},(\mathfrak{st}_{2},M^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
.
Similarly,
(bisim
2
)
implies that for every
\mathfrak{st}_{2}^{\prime}\in M_{i}^{\prime}
there is a
\mathfrak{st}_{2}\in M_{i}
such that
(\mathfrak{st}_{2},(\mathfrak{st}_{2},M^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
. Hence, in this case
M^{\prime}
is an
S
-successor of
M
.

As a second case, suppose there are no
\mathfrak{st}_{1}\in M_{i}
,
\mathfrak{st}_{1}^{\prime}\in M_{i}^{\prime}
and
j_{1},j_{1}^{\prime}<\omega
such that
((\mathfrak{st}_{1},j_{1}),(\mathfrak{st}_{1}^{\prime},j^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
. Then for every such
\mathfrak{st},\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}
we have
((\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\not\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
.

These cases are exhaustive, so the
(bisim)
condition is satisfied.

(wit
1
)

Suppose
\mathfrak{st}=(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})\in M_{i}
and
\Diamond^{0}_{S}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}_{c}
. Then, because
S^{M,\mathcal{M})}
satisfies
(witness
1
)
, every
(\mathfrak{st},j)
has
>0

S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
-successors
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},j^{\prime},M^{\prime})
. Therefore,
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime})
is an
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
successor of
\mathfrak{st}
. Hence
(wit
1
)
is satisfied.

(wit
2
)

As with
(wit
1
)
, except here we use the fact that
S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
satisfies
(witness
2
)
.

(wit
3
)

Assume
R\in\varrho
and
S\in\{R,R^{-}\}
.
Take any
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
. Let
\mathfrak{st}=(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})\in M_{i}
. Fix any
j,j^{\prime}<\omega
such that
((\mathfrak{st},j),(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},j^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
. For every
M^{\ast}=(M_{1}^{\ast},M_{2}^{\ast})\in\mathcal{M}
, we say that
M^{\ast}
is an
S
-successor mosaic of
(\mathfrak{st},j)
if there are
\mathfrak{st}^{\ast},j^{\ast}
such that
((\mathfrak{st},j),(\mathfrak{st}^{\ast},j^{\ast},M^{\ast}))\in S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
.

Let
g
be any function that (1) assigns to every
S
-successor mosaic
M^{\ast}
of
(\mathfrak{st},j)
an
\mathfrak{st}^{\ast}
such that there is a
j^{\ast}
with
((\mathfrak{st},j),(\mathfrak{st}^{\ast},j^{\ast},M^{\ast}))\in S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
and (2) satisfies
g(M^{\prime})=\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}
.

As
S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
satisfies
(witness
1
)
, we have that if
\Diamond_{S}^{=n}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}_{C}
then there are at most
n
mosaics
M^{\ast}
such that
g(M^{\ast})=\mathfrak{st}^{\ast}=(t^{\ast},\mathfrak{s}^{\ast}_{p},\mathfrak{s}^{\ast}_{c})
for some
\mathfrak{s}^{\ast}_{p},\mathfrak{s}^{\ast}_{c}
. Furthermore, because of the
(bisim
1
)
condition, the set of
S
-successor mosaics does not depend on the choice of
j
.

It follows that
g
is a function that satisfies the requirements of
(wit
3
)
, so that property is satisfied for
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
.

We have now shown that
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
satisfies all of the conditions for
\mathcal{M}
to be a syntactic witness of
M
.

(\Leftarrow)
Suppose
\mathcal{M}
is a syntactic witness for
M
and the elements of
\Gamma\cup\Delta
are enumerated. For
R\in\sigma
and
S\in\{R,R^{-}\}
, let
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
be the witnessing relation. We will create the relation
S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
by adding
S
- and
S^{-}
-edges to elements
x\in\Delta\cup\Gamma
in the enumeration order. Throughout the process, we keep invariant the property that, by the time we reach
x\in\Delta\cup\Gamma
, there is at most one
S
- or
S^{-1}
-edge to or from
x
already. Four cases are possible.

Case
1:
x=(\mathfrak{st},j)\in\Delta
,
\mathfrak{st}=(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})
, and no edges from
x
have been added yet. First, suppose
R\in\sigma\setminus\varrho
. For every type
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
, if
\Diamond^{=n}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}_{c}
for some
n>0
, we fix any
M^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime})
such that
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
. (At least one such
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime})
exists due to
(wit
1
)
.) Now add
S
-edges from
x
to the first
n
elements of the form
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},k,M^{\prime})\in\Gamma
that do not yet have any edges to it. Similarly, if
\Diamond^{>k^{\bullet}}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}_{c}
, then add
S
-edges from
x
to the lowest (in the enumeration)
k^{\bullet}+1
elements
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},k,M^{\prime})\in\Gamma
that do not yet have edges to it.
This construction ensures that
(witness
1
)
will be satisfied for
S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
.

Now, suppose
R\in\varrho
. In this case, our construction has to be more complex, since we need to satisfy not only
(witness
1
)
but also
(bisim
1
)
. Let
\mathcal{M}_{S}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
be the set of
S
-successors of
M
in the sense defined in
(bisim)
. This means that we must ensure that, for every
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{S}
, there is at least one
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},j^{\prime},M^{\prime})\in\Gamma
such that
(x,(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},j^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
, while obeying the number restrictions. To this purpose, fix any
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime})
such that
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
. Let
g
be the function given by
(wit
3
)
and let
\mathcal{E}=\{(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime},M^{\prime\prime})\mid g(M^{\prime\prime})=\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime}\}
.

Consider any
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\Diamond^{=n}_{S}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}_{c}
. It is a property of
g
that there are at most
n
elements in
\mathcal{E}
that have a
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime\prime}
component. If there are less than
n
such elements, take any
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime},M^{\prime\prime})
such that
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime},M^{\prime\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
, and add enough copies of this
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime},M^{\prime\prime})
to
\mathcal{E}
to make the total number of elements with a
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime\prime}
component exactly
n
. We turn
\mathcal{E}
into a multi-set by doing this. (Such
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime},M^{\prime\prime})
must exist by
(wit
1
)
.)

Similarly, if
\Diamond^{>k^{\bullet}}_{S}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}_{c}
and there are
\leq k^{\bullet}
elements in
\mathcal{E}
with a
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime\prime}
component, add copies of
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime},M^{\prime\prime})
until we have more than
k^{\bullet}
such elements.

Now, for every
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime})\in\mathcal{E}
, add an
S
-edge from
x=(\mathfrak{st},j)
to the lowest element
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},k,M^{\prime})\in\Gamma
that does not yet have any edges to it. This construction ensures that
(witness
1
)
and
(bisim
1
)
will be satisfied for
S^{M,\mathcal{M}}
.

Case
2:
x=(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},k,M^{\prime})\in\Gamma
and no edges to
x
have been added yet. For every
S
, we do the following. If
\Diamond^{=n}_{S^{-}}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}_{p}
or
\Diamond^{>k^{\bullet}}_{S^{-}}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}_{p}
, let
\mathfrak{st}
be such that
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
. Create
S
edges to
x
from the first
n
(if
\Diamond^{=n}_{S^{-}}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}_{p}
) or the first
k^{\bullet}+1
(if
\Diamond^{>k^{\bullet}}_{S^{-}}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}_{p}
) elements of the form
(\mathfrak{st},j)\in\Delta
that do not yet have any outgoing edges.

Case
3: Suppose
x=(\mathfrak{st},j)\in\Delta
and there is already an
S
edge from
x
to
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},k,M^{\prime})\in\Gamma
. Add edges for
S^{-1}
as in case 1. With regard to
S
, add edges like in case 1 except that instead of taking any
S
-successor we now take the successor
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime})
as a starting point, and take the already existing
S
edge from
(\mathfrak{st},j)
to
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},k,M^{\prime})
instead of one of the edges that we would otherwise add.

Case
4: Suppose
x=(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},k,M^{\prime})\in\Gamma
and there is already an
S
edge from
(\mathfrak{st},j)
to
x
. Add edges for
S^{-1}
as in case 2. For
S
, do the same except we take the edge from
(\mathfrak{st},j)
to
x
instead of one edge that would otherwise be added.

It is immediate from the construction that conditions
(witness
1
)
,
(witness
2
)
and
(bisim
1
)
are satisfied; condition
(bisim
2
)
follows from
\mathcal{M}
being a syntactic witness.

\dashv

Witnesses for mosaics defined by (disjoint) structures
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{A}_{2}
can be extracted as follows. If
M=M(\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2},a,\emptyset)
is a root mosaic, then

\mathcal{M}=\{M(\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2},b,a)\mid b\in N(a^{\prime}),\text{ for some }a^{\prime}\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}(\varrho)}a\}

is a witness for
M
. If
M=M(\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2},a,p)
, for
a,p\in{\sf dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{i})
, then we obtain a witness
\mathcal{M}
for
M
by taking

\mathcal{M}=\{M(\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2},b,a)\mid\mathfrak{A}_{i},b\not\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{A}_{i},p,\ b\in N(a^{\prime}),\\
\text{ for some }a^{\prime}\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}(\varrho)}a\}.

Alternative and possibly larger witnesses can be obtained by dropping the restrictions ‘
b\in N(a^{\prime})
, for some
a^{\prime}\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}(\varrho)}a
’​.
In both cases, we call
\mathcal{M}
a
witness for
M
given by
\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2}
.

It is rather straightforward to see now that there exist
\mathfrak{A}_{i},a_{i}
,
i=1,2
, such that
\mathfrak{A}_{i}\models\varphi_{i}(a_{i})
and
\mathfrak{A}_{1},a_{1}\sim_{\mathsf{ML\!^{i,u}}(\varrho)}\mathfrak{A}_{2},a_{2}
iff there is a set
\mathfrak{S}
of mosaics satisfying
(m1)
–
(m4)
.
So we focus on showing that it is enough to use mosaics and witnesses of exponential size.

Call successor sets
\mathfrak{s}
and
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}

profile-equivalent
if they have the same
S
-profiles for all
S\in\{R,R^{-}\}
with
R\in\sigma
. Star-types
\mathfrak{st}=(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})
and
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime})
are called
parent profile-equivalent
if
\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{p}
,
\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime}
are profile equivalent, and
child profile-equivalent
if
\mathfrak{t}=\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{c}
,
\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime}
are profile equivalent.
Mosaics
M=(M_{1},M_{2})
and
M^{\prime}=(M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})
are called
child profile-equivalent
if, for any
\mathfrak{st}\in M_{i}
, there exists a child-profile equivalent
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}\in M_{i}^{\prime}
, and vice versa, for
i=1,2
;
M
and
M^{\prime}
are called
parent profile-equivalent
if, for any
\mathfrak{st}\in M_{i}
, there exists a parent-profile equivalent
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}\in M_{i}^{\prime}
, and vice versa, for
i=1,2
.
Observe that the number of child/parent profile non-equivalent star-types does not exceed
(|\varphi_{1}|+|\varphi_{2}|)^{4(|\varphi_{1}|+|\varphi_{2}|)}
.

See
15

Proof.

We proceed in three steps. First, for every mosaic
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
, we reduce the number of star-types in
M^{\prime}
to an at most exponential amount. Second, we reduce the number of mosaics in
\mathcal{M}
. Finally, we reduce the number of star-types in
M
. After each of these steps, we show that the result is still a syntactic witness.

Step
1.
For each relation
S
, let
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
be the witnessing relation for
M
and
\mathcal{M}
, and consider any
M^{\prime}=(M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{M}
. There can be arbitrarily many star-types in
M_{1}^{\prime}\cup M_{2}^{\prime}
. We want to select subsets
N_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq M_{1}^{\prime}
and
N_{2}^{\prime}\subseteq M_{2}^{\prime}
of at most exponential size. Importantly, however, we want to do so in such a way that
(i)

N^{\prime}=(N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime})
is child profile-equivalent to
M^{\prime}
and
(ii)
every edge
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
, where
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}
is not included in
N^{\prime}
, can be redirected to some
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime},N^{\prime}))
, while still satisfying the conditions of a witnessing relation.

The first of these requirements will be important when we eventually use our collection of mosaics to build a model; because of this child profile-equivalence we will be able to replace
M^{\prime}
with
N^{\prime}
in a model construction. The second requirement means that the resulting set of mosaics remains a syntactic witness for
M
.

Formally, for any
M^{\prime}=(M_{1},M_{2})\in\mathcal{M}
, let
N^{\prime}=(N_{1}^{\prime},N_{2}^{\prime})
be any mosaic such that
(i)

N_{i}^{\prime}\subseteq M_{i}^{\prime}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
(ii)

M^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime}
are child profile-equivalent,

(iii)
for every
\mathfrak{t}
,
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
and
S
, if there is a
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}_{p},\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}_{c})\in M_{i}^{\prime}
such that
\Diamond_{S}^{>0}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}_{p}
then there is a
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime}=(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime\prime})\in N_{i}^{\prime}
such that
\Diamond_{S}^{>0}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}_{p}
and
(iv)

|N_{1}|+|N_{2}|\leq k_{\varphi_{1}\varphi_{2}}
.

Note that while
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime}
may have different parent and child profiles, it must be based on the same type
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
as
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}
. Such
N^{\prime}
exists because
N_{1}^{\prime}
and
N_{2}^{\prime}
need to contain, in the worst case, one element for each child profile (to ensure child profile-equivalence) and one further element for each
S
and each pair of types. There are exponentially many child profiles, exponentially many types and polynomially many relations, so we can select
N_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq M_{1}^{\prime}
and
N_{2}^{\prime}\subseteq M_{2}^{\prime}
of the required size.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be the result of replacing each
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
by the corresponding
N^{\prime}
. We show that
\mathcal{K}
is a syntactic witness for
M
by constructing the witnessing relation
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{K}}
. For any
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
, if
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}\in N^{\prime}
, then we simply take
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{K}}
. If
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}\not\in N^{\prime}
, let
\mathfrak{st}=(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})
and
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime})
.

Because of the
(coherence)
condition, we have
\mathfrak{st}\rightsquigarrow_{S}\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}
and therefore, in particular,
\Diamond^{>0}_{S^{-}}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}_{p}
. By construction, there must be some
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime}=(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime\prime})\in N^{\prime}
such that
\Diamond_{S^{-}}\mathfrak{t}\in\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}_{p}
, which implies that
\mathfrak{st}\rightsquigarrow_{S}\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime}
. We then take
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime},N^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{K}}
.

It is straightforward to verify that this
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{K}}
satisfies all of the requirements for
\mathcal{K}
being a syntactic witness for
M
:

(coherence)

holds because any
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},N^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{K}}
either existed in
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
or was redirected to some
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}
with the property that
\mathfrak{st}\rightsquigarrow_{S}\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}
,

(bisim) and (wit
3
)

hold because we redirected edges only within the same mosaic, so
N^{\prime}
is an
S
-successor of
M
(w.r.t.
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{K}}
) iff
M^{\prime}
is an
S
-successor of
M
(w.r.t.
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{M}}
),

(wit
1
) and (wit
2
)

hold because we redirected edges only to star-types based on the same type.

Step
2.
Now, we select a subset
\mathcal{N}\subseteq\mathcal{K}
of at most exponential size, while it remains a syntactic witness for
M
. In order to do this, we redirect edges from
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{K}}
. Importantly, we redirect an edge
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},N^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{K}}
only to
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},N^{\prime\prime})
where
N^{\prime\prime}
is already an
S
-successor of
M
with respect to
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{K}}
.

Formally, let
\mathcal{N}
be any subset of
\mathcal{K}
such that
(i)
for every
S
, every
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\mathcal{N}
, every
i\in\{1,2\}
, every
\mathfrak{st}=(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})\in M_{i}
and every
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime})\in M_{i}^{\prime}
, if
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{K}}
then there are
M^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
and
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime}=(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime\prime})\in M_{i}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\mathfrak{st}\rightsquigarrow_{S}\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime}
and
M_{i}^{\prime\prime}
is an
S
-successor of
M
w.r.t.
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{K}}
and
(ii)

|\mathcal{N}|\leq k_{\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}}
.
Such
\mathcal{N}
exists because, in the worst case,
\mathcal{N}
needs to contain one mosaic for every
i
, every
S
and every
\mathfrak{st},\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}
, of which there are exponentially many.

We show that
\mathcal{N}
is a syntactic witness for
M
by constructing the witnessing relation
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{N}}
from the relation
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{K}}
. For any
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{K}}
, if
M^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
, then we simply take
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{N}}
. If
M^{\prime}\not\in\mathcal{N}
, then by construction there are an
S
-successor
M^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{N}
of
M
such that
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}\in M^{\prime\prime}_{1}\cup M^{\prime\prime}_{2}
. We take
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{N}}
.

It is again straightforward to verify that all of the requirements for a syntactic witness are satisfied:

(coherence), (wit
1
) and (wit
2
)

hold because we an edge to
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime})
was redirected to
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},M^{\prime\prime})
, i.e., the same star-type but in a different mosaic,

(bisim) and (wit
3
)

hold because we redirected edges only to mosaics that were already
S
-successors of
M
.

Step
3. Finally, we select subsets
N_{1}\subseteq M_{1}
and
N_{2}\subseteq M_{2}
such that
(i)

N=(N_{1},N_{2})
is parent profile-equivalent to
M
and
(ii)
every edge
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},N^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{N}}
can be replaced by an edge
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},N^{\prime}))
where
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime}\in N_{1}\cup N_{2}
.

Formally, let
N=(N_{1},N_{2})
be any mosaic such that
(i)

N_{i}\subseteq M_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
(ii)

M
and
N
are parent profile-equivalent,
(iii)
for every
\mathfrak{t}
,
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
and
S
, if there is a
(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})\in M_{i}
such that
\Diamond^{>0}_{S}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}_{c}
, then there is a
(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime\prime})\in N_{i}
such that
\Diamond_{S}^{>0}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}_{c}
and
(iv)

|N_{1}|+|N_{2}|\leq k_{\varphi_{1}\varphi_{2}}
.

We show that
\mathcal{N}
is a syntactic witness for
N
by constructing the witnessing relation
\bar{S}^{N,\mathcal{N}}
. For any
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},N^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{N}}
, if
\mathfrak{st}\in N
, then we take
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},N^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{N,\mathcal{N}}
. If
\mathfrak{st}\not\in N
, take
(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},N^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{N,\mathcal{N}}
, where
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime}=(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime\prime})
is the star-type such that
\Diamond_{S}^{>0}\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}_{c}
. Note that in this case
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime}\rightsquigarrow_{S}\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}
.

This
\bar{S}^{N,\mathcal{N}}
satisfies all of the requirements of a syntactic witness:

(coherence)

holds because any edge
(\mathfrak{st},(\mathfrak{st}^{\prime},N^{\prime}))\in\bar{S}^{N,\mathcal{N}}
either was in
\bar{S}^{M,\mathcal{N}}
or had
\mathfrak{st}
chosen in such a way that
\mathfrak{st}\rightsquigarrow_{S}\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}
,

(wit
1
) and (wit
2
)

hold because
\mathfrak{st}
and
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime\prime}
are based on the same type
\mathfrak{t}
,

(bisim) and (wit
3
)

hold because we did not add any new
S
-successors.

We have now shown that
\mathcal{N}
is a syntactic witness for
N
. Furthermore,
N
and
\mathcal{N}
satisfy all of the requirements of the lemma.

\dashv

We are now in a position to formulate the mosaic elimination procedure establishing the
2\textsc{ExpTime}
upper bound in Theorem
10
. Let
\mathfrak{S}_{0}
be the set of all mosaics
M=(M_{1},M_{2})
with non-empty
M_{1}
,
M_{2}
and
|M_{1}\cup M_{2}|\leq k_{\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}}^{2}
. Clearly
\mathfrak{S}_{0}
can be computed in double exponential time in
|\varphi_{1}|+|\varphi_{2}|
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}_{0}
. We call
M\in\mathfrak{S}

bad
in
\mathfrak{S}
if at least one of the following conditions does not hold:

–

there exists a syntactic witness
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathfrak{S}
for
M
such that
|\mathcal{M}|\leq k_{\varphi_{1}\varphi_{2}}
;

–

if
\Diamondblack\chi\in\mathfrak{t}
and
(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})\in M_{i}
, for
M=(M_{1},M_{2})\in\mathfrak{S}
and
i\in\{1,2\}
, then there is a root mosaic
M^{\prime}=(M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{S}
such that
\chi\in\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
, for some
(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime})\in M_{i}^{\prime}
, with
\mathfrak{t}
and
\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}
being
\Diamondblack
-equivalent.

We compute a sequence
\mathfrak{S}_{0},\mathfrak{S}_{1},\dots
, where
\mathfrak{S}_{i+1}
is obtained from
\mathfrak{S}_{i}
by eliminating all bad mosaics, and stop when
\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}=\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, setting
\mathfrak{S}^{\ast}=\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. Clearly,
\mathfrak{S}^{\ast}
is computable in double exponential time.

See
16

Proof.

(i)\Rightarrow(ii)
Suppose
\mathfrak{A}_{i},a_{i}
,
i=1,2
, satisfying
(i)
are given. Consider the set of all pairs
p=(M,\mathcal{M}_{M})
such that
\mathcal{M}_{M}

\mathfrak{A}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{A}_{2}

\leq k_{\varphi_{1}\varphi_{2}}

and obtain pairs

satisfying the conditions of the lemma. However, these pairs are not necessarily defined by
\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2}
. We call the
M^{p}

child-good
and all mosaics in any
\mathcal{M}_{M}^{p}

parent-good
(as we have found the relevant witnesses for graded modalities in children and, respectively, parents).

Given a parent good mosaic
M=(M_{1},M_{2})
and a child-good mosaic
M^{\prime}=(M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}^{\prime})
that are parent- and child-profile equivalent, we construct a new mosaic
M|M^{\prime}=(M_{1}|M_{1}^{\prime},M_{2}|M_{2}^{\prime})
which, for any parent- and child-profile equivalent star-types
\mathfrak{st}=(\mathfrak{t},\mathfrak{s}_{p},\mathfrak{s}_{c})\in M_{i}
and
\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{t}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{p}^{\prime},\mathfrak{s}_{c}^{\prime})\in M_{i}^{\prime}

\mathfrak{st}|\mathfrak{st}^{\prime}

we obtain the required root mosaic in

.

.
We use

Lemmas

,

, and

give the
